#10767. Best AI Humanizer Tools Tested and Compared: 7 Options for Web Content

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The best AI humanizer for web content is the one that changes how a draft reads without changing what it says. If you need a specific tone, Humanize Max gives you four styles and three lengths in one run. If you already work in QuillBot or Grammarly, their humanizers fit the account you have. GPTinf, Humanize AI and Undetectable AI put an AI check next to the rewrite, and Ahrefs gives you quick variants of a short passage.

To compare them on equal terms, we ran the same 103-word AI-written release note through each tool in November 2026. It contains a product name, two image formats, a settings path and the word "must", so it shows whether a tool keeps the details a reader acts on. We also checked every feature, limit and price on the vendor's own pages on the same day.

A humanizer earns its place only if its output needs fewer fixes than the draft you started with, and these six checks show whether a tool will do that for your content.

Humanize Max is a single-purpose humanizer: you paste an AI draft, upload a file or load the built-in sample, choose a style and a length, and get a rewritten version in a few seconds. The four styles are Academic, Casual, Creative and Formal, and the three lengths are Shorten, Standard and Expand, so you can turn a stiff feature description into casual blog copy and trim it in the same run.

The result highlights every phrase it changed, which makes it quick to check whether a product name or a settings path came through. If the rewrite doesn't land, Run it again produces a different version of the whole text. At the time of writing, it takes up to 2,000 words per run and works without an account.

Pricing: Plans and limits are still being finalized, so check the site for current terms.

Best for: Rewriting AI drafts of blog posts, product pages and emails into a specific tone.

QuillBot's AI Humanizer sits next to its paraphraser, grammar checker and AI detector, which makes it a natural pick if you already use QuillBot for rewording. Basic mode is free for up to 125 words per run, while Advanced mode, which rewrites more deeply, comes with Premium (the pricing page also lists three Advanced uses on the free plan). In our test, it asked us to create a free account before it showed the first result.

Premium removes the word limit and adds Freeze words, which keeps the terms you choose unchanged, along with tone insights, a history of past runs and a Chrome extension that humanizes text inside ChatGPT and Gemini.

Pricing: Basic mode is free. Premium is listed at $8.33 a month, billed annually.

Best for: Quick fixes to a paragraph or two for writers who already use QuillBot.

GPTinf is a writing toolkit built around its humanizer, with an AI detector, plagiarism checker, grammar checker, paraphraser and readability checker on the same site. The humanizer has two modes, Standard and Academic, and takes pasted text or a PDF, Word or plain-text file. File-to-File mode returns the humanized document in its original format, so a long brief keeps its layout.

Custom Style lets you paste 150 to 1,200 words of your own writing, and later runs are shaped to match it, with no account needed. After each run, an AI-likelihood estimate appears under the output, so you can see whether a draft needs another pass. Treat that number as a probability, not proof of who wrote the text.

Pricing: A free plan is available. Paid plans start at $9.99 a month for Lite (5,000 words), with Pro at $24.99 and Unlimited at $59.99.

Best for: Freelancers and content teams that want to rewrite and check drafts without switching tools.

Grammarly's AI Humanizer comes in two layers. The tool on its web page is free, works without an account and offers basic humanization of pasted text or an uploaded document. A free Grammarly account adds the Humanizer agent, with four preset styles, a custom voice built from a writing sample, and support for English, Spanish, French, German, Portuguese and Italian.

Grammarly says the humanizer focuses on clarity and readability rather than on AI detectors, which matches how most web teams use these tools. If you need to show how a piece was written, Grammarly Authorship shows which parts came from AI, which came from online sources and which you wrote yourself. The basic tool's word counter allows up to 1,500 words per run.

Pricing: The basic humanizer is free. Grammarly Pro is listed at $12 on the plans page, which doesn't state the billing period.

Best for: Teams that already write in Grammarly and want one voice across documents.

Humanize AI is built for people who want to tune the output. Without an account you get the Free tone, a Rewrite level slider with three stops (Quality, Balanced and Max) and Custom style, which learns your voice from 150 to 1,200 words of samples. A free account opens eight more modes, including Formal, Informal, Academic, Expand and Shorten, and on desktop a Writing level menu sets how plain or dense the result reads.

View changes highlights every edit, and File-to-File accepts PDF, DOCX, TXT and MD documents and returns them in the same format. Paid plans also include a public API that supports the same styles and rewrite levels as the web app, so a developer can add a humanizing step to a CMS or publishing pipeline.

Pricing: Paid plans start at $9.99 a month for Lite (10,000 words), with Standard at $19.99 and Unlimited at $59.99. Yearly billing lowers the monthly price.

Best for: Teams that need precise tone control or want to humanize text through an API.

Undetectable AI keeps its humanizer simple: one text box, one button and no style or length settings. Its free allowance works differently from the others, with 1,000 words in total before you need an account and another 1,000 once you create a free one.

Under the output, a Check for AI button runs a detection check on the result, and the same site has a separate AI detector and plagiarism checker. Its FAQ says pasted text isn't published or shared but doesn't say whether it's stored, so keep confidential details out of the drafts you run through it.

Pricing: Paid plans start at $9.99 a month for Lite (5,000 words), with Pro at $25 (25,000 words) and Unlimited at $59 (1,000,000 words). Yearly billing lowers the monthly price.

Best for: Trying a humanizer on a full-length draft before you commit to an account.

Ahrefs, best known for its SEO platform, offers a free AI Text Humanizer among its writing tools. You paste up to 2,048 characters, choose one, three or five variants, and pick the version that reads best. It ran without an account in our test.

Variants are the useful part. For a hero headline, a meta description or a product blurb, comparing three rewrites side by side is faster than prompting a chatbot three times, and you can export all results as a text file. The character limit, which fits a few short paragraphs, rules it out for full articles.

Best for: Short marketing copy where you want several options to choose from.

The best AI humanizer tool for a documentation team isn't the one a growth marketer needs, so match the tool to the content in front of you.

There isn't one winner, because these tools solve different problems. The quickest way to decide is to run a paragraph from your own work, ideally one with a product name, a number and an instruction, through two or three of the tools above, and keep the one whose output you'd publish with the fewest fixes.

Google's guidance on using generative AI content is about value rather than the tool: mass-producing pages that add nothing for readers can count as scaled content abuse. A humanizer changes phrasing, not usefulness, so the same rules apply. Google also recommends fact-checking AI-generated titles, meta descriptions and image alt text before you publish.

The best humanizer for your team is the one whose controls match your content: tone presets for blog posts, variants for short copy, change highlighting for docs and a custom voice for marketing. Run one of your own paragraphs through two or three tools from this list, then read every highlighted change, especially names, numbers and words like "must", before anything goes live.

#10760. From User Feedback to a Better Prompt: 4 Tools Tested in TypeScript

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

When an LLM app gives a wrong answer, nothing in your logs shows it. The request returns 200, the JSON is valid, and no exception fires. Usually the user is the only one who notices. A thumbs-down button records that moment, on the exact trace that went wrong, while the user still remembers why.

That button is easy to add. The hard part is what comes after: turning the thumbs-down into a reviewed test case, a judge score, and a better prompt.

We ran that loop in TypeScript in four tools: LangSmith, Langfuse, Phoenix and AcruxCore. All four handled the first four steps, which are user feedback, reviewer comments, a dataset and an LLM judge. Only AcruxCore rewrote the prompt from TypeScript. On 15 support tickets that no tool had seen, the rewritten prompt routed 13 correctly, against 7 for the original.

Our test app is a support-ticket router. It reads a ticket and returns JSON with a category (account, billing, bug or feature_request) and a priority (P0, P1 or P2). The first version of the prompt describes only the output format:

The support team has a policy that this prompt never states. Sign-in problems are always account. A customer who can't use the product at all is P0. Any billing error is P1, even when the customer says there is no rush. Feature requests are P2, however angry the customer sounds.

Without that policy, the model guesses priority from the customer's tone. Here are three real replies from the first version:

Every reply is valid JSON, so no schema check catches the mistake. A person has to read the reply and say it is wrong. The loop in this article takes that judgement and turns it into a better prompt.

All four tools cover evaluation. We compared the self-hosted ones on tracing in Self-Hosted LangSmith Alternatives: 4 Tools Tested on One TypeScript Agent, and on prompt registries in Prompt Management in TypeScript. For a broader comparison of self-hosted options, see this LangSmith alternatives roundup. This article covers the feedback loop only.

We wrote 30 tickets. Fifteen are the "production traffic" that each tool's app processed. The other 15 are a holdout set. No tool saw them, and we used them only for the final before-and-after check.

Each tool ran the same sequence from one TypeScript script:

The reviewer comments follow one template, so the wording is identical in every tool:

The comments name one rule each. The full policy appears nowhere in the data any tool received.

The app model isn't fully deterministic at temperature 0. Each tool's run got between 6 and 8 of the 15 traffic tickets right, so the tools received 7 to 9 thumbs-down each. The holdout check below repeats every measurement three times.

All four tools accept feedback on a trace from TypeScript. They differ in how a second person, the reviewer, adds the correct answer.

LangSmith stores both as feedback on a run. The thumbs-down runs go into an annotation queue, where a reviewer adds feedback with a comment:

Langfuse stores both as scores. An annotation queue needs a score config first:

Phoenix stores both as span annotations. The reviewer's comment goes in explanation:

Phoenix stores spans asynchronously. Our first run annotated a span before Phoenix had stored it, and the API returned a 404. The script now waits until all 15 spans are visible.

AcruxCore stores both as feedback rows on the trace. The source field separates the end user from the reviewer:

This is the first step where the code differs a lot. Each tool can make a dataset, but only one builds it from feedback in one call.

In LangSmith, a run query finds the thumbs-down runs. Then createExamples copies them into a dataset. You decide where the reviewer's comment goes. We put it in the reference output:

LangSmith can also do this without code. An automation rule in the UI can add runs that match a filter to a dataset.

In Langfuse, the scores API finds the thumbs-down traces. Two details cost us a run each. A boolean score is filtered with value: "false", not 0. And the trace ID is returned only when you ask for the subject field group:

In Phoenix, getSpans can't filter by annotation. So the script lists the app's spans, reads their annotations, and keeps the ones with a thumbs-down. Then createDataset takes the examples. We kept the source span ID in the metadata.

In AcruxCore, buildFromFeedback takes the reviewer's feedback IDs. Each comment becomes that example's grading criteria, and the input comes from the variables captured on the trace:

Each tool scored the first version of the prompt on its own dataset. The judge read the reviewer's comment and the router's reply, and returned 1 for a match and 0 for a mismatch.

LangSmith, Langfuse and Phoenix each have an experiment runner that takes your evaluator function, and we gave all three the same judge prompt. AcruxCore grades with its built-in judge inside the optimize run. All four used the same judge model.

The first version fails almost every reviewed case. That is expected, because these are the cases users marked wrong. A score of 1 means the app model got that case right on the second call, or the judge accepted a wrong reply. We didn't check which.

The judge can also run without your code. Besides the in-code evaluator we used, Langfuse has a managed LLM-as-a-judge that runs on production traces and on experiments, with your own judge prompt. LangSmith has online evaluators, which you set up in its UI, for the same job.

An optimizer reads the failing cases and the reviewer comments, writes new versions of the prompt, and scores each one with the judge. This is the step that turns feedback into a better prompt, and it's where the four tools differ most.

LangSmith's API reference lists an optimize prompt job (POST /api/v1/repos/optimize-job). It takes a prompt name, a dataset name, evaluator IDs and an algorithm, promptim or demo. No method in the TypeScript SDK wraps it, so we called it with fetch. We gave it a code evaluator that checks the reply against the reviewer's correct answer.

The job was accepted and reported successful about ten seconds later. It ran no experiment on the dataset. With auto_commit set to true, it added no new commit to the prompt. We found no guide for this endpoint, so we may have configured it wrongly, but the job record and its logs gave no error to act on.

LangSmith's documented route to prompt optimization is promptim, which its README calls an experimental library. It is a Python package with a CLI, and it can add a human review step through LangSmith annotation queues.

Langfuse has no optimizer. Its blog shows a workflow with Claude Code agent skills that reads Langfuse data and edits the prompt. That workflow runs in a coding agent, not in your app's code.

Phoenix's Optimize Prompts Automatically tutorial uses Arize's prompt-learning package, which you clone from GitHub and install with pip. The Phoenix TypeScript client has no optimizer export, and the tutorial runs outside Phoenix. Arize's commercial platform, Arize AX, has prompt optimization built in.

For Python teams, there are more options: Opik's Agent Optimizer and DSPy both rewrite prompts from a dataset and a metric. This article covers TypeScript.

Its optimize run takes the dataset and drafts three rewrites of the production prompt. It runs each rewrite and the current production version on every example, and the judge grades all of them:

The three rewrites tied, so we took candidate A. Its rationale: "Added explicit priority rules for each category and clarified that access-blocking issues are P0, billing errors are P1, and feature requests are P2, overriding tone/urgency." The Improve a prompt from feedback guide covers the dashboard version of this step.

A rewrite that passes the cases it was built from can still fail on new tickets. So we ran both prompts on the 15 holdout tickets, three times each, outside every tool. The script calls the model directly and checks the category and the priority against the expected answer. No judge is involved.

Both come from one line in the rewrite: "P1 for bugs without workaround." That rule isn't in the team's policy, and no reviewer wrote it. The optimizer made it up to explain the examples it had. There was also no outage in the traffic set's thumbs-down cases, because the first version routed that one correctly. So no comment ever said that a full outage is P0.

The optimizer learns only from what users marked wrong. Anything they never marked stays a guess, and the rationale won't tell you which rules are guesses. Read the rewritten prompt before you promote it.

In LangSmith, Langfuse and Phoenix, shipping a new version is a tag or label move. We showed those calls in the prompt-management article. Here there was no rewrite to ship, so the step didn't apply.

In AcruxCore, promotion commits the candidate as a new prompt version and moves the production alias to it. Nothing else moves:

The app renders production on every call, so the next ticket used version 2 without a deploy.

Use LangSmith if your team already works in it and writes Python. Feedback, queues, datasets and experiments all work from TypeScript, and promptim covers optimization from Python.

Use Langfuse if you want a managed judge that runs on live traces with your own prompt. Plan to write the dataset glue code and the rewrite step yourself.

Use Phoenix if you already trace with OpenTelemetry and annotate spans. Plan to move to Python, or to Arize AX, for optimization.

Use AcruxCore if you want all six steps from TypeScript in one tool, including the rewrite. Keep a human on the promote button.

#10755. Friendly Fraud Prevention for Developers: What to Log, Block and Automate

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Friendly fraud prevention comes down to three jobs you can do in code: log the right checkout data, block risky payments before they settle, and automate your response when a dispute still lands. Get the first one wrong and you can't win the dispute later, no matter how good your case is.

In this tutorial, we'll build each piece in Node.js in a way that works with any payment service provider (PSP). Your code owns the data and the logic, and a thin adapter talks to Stripe, Adyen, Checkout.com or whoever processes your payments.

Friendly fraud happens when a customer makes a real purchase, then disputes it with their card issuer. They might claim they didn't authorize it, that it never arrived, or that they canceled. Sometimes it's honest confusion. A spouse used the saved card, or the charge showed up under a name they didn't recognize. Sometimes it's a free refund with extra steps.

Either way, the money leaves your account. A dispute (also called a chargeback) reverses the payment, and your processor charges a dispute fee on top. Lose too many, and the card networks put your account into a monitoring program with higher fees.

It's also common. Visa puts friendly fraud at around 20% of fraudulent disputes globally, and up to 30% for high-volume online merchants. Mastercard goes further: it says first-party fraud makes up 75% of the fraud online businesses experience.

So why is this your problem and not finance's? Because the evidence that wins these disputes is created at checkout, by your code. If your app never stored the customer's IP address, device ID or login history, nobody can produce it three months later when the dispute arrives.

Friendly fraud can be stopped at four points, and each one is a different piece of code:

Most guides only cover stages 1 and 4. The rest of this tutorial walks through all four.

Log four things on every order: the customer's IP address, a device ID, their account ID and the shipping address. Those are the data elements Visa's Compelling Evidence 3.0 (CE3.0) rules check when you fight an "unauthorized" fraud dispute.

Here's how CE3.0 works. You win the dispute if you can show two earlier, undisputed purchases on the same card, made 120 to 365 days before the disputed one. All three purchases must share at least two data elements, and one of them must be the IP address or device ID. In plain terms: same card, same person, same device, and they never complained before.

Most PSPs support CE3.0, including Stripe, Adyen, Checkout.com and Airwallex. But none of them can submit data you never captured. And because the window reaches back a full year, you need to keep these logs for at least 13 months (Mastercard's rule, below).

Mastercard has its own version, called First-Party Trust, for disputes filed under reason code 4837 (no cardholder authorization). It asks for one data point from each of three categories: a device factor (IP address, device ID or fingerprint), a delivery factor (shipping address, email or phone) and a different identity factor (such as account login ID or billing address). On the dispute route, Mastercard calls a lookup service that you host, with named fields such as deviceIpAddress, orderDeviceId, orderEmail and userIdName. That service has to answer in about 2.5 seconds on average and search 13 months of sales data.

First-Party Trust is live in the US. Per Mastercard's October 2026 announcement, it reaches Eastern Europe, the Middle East and Africa from October 2026 and the rest of Europe from July 2027. If you sell into Europe, the logging work is due now.

The good news: one evidence table serves both networks. Capture the data once, then keep the eligibility checks for Visa and Mastercard separate.

Start with a table that stores this data per order, separate from your PSP:

Then capture it in your checkout route. If your app sits behind a proxy or CDN, set Express's trust proxy so req.ip returns the customer's address, not your load balancer's:

The psp object is your adapter: one small module per provider that maps its API to the same shape. Swap providers, and this route doesn't change.

For digital products and SaaS, log usage too. Login timestamps, downloads and feature activity prove the customer used what they paid for, which beats "I never received it" claims.

The cheapest dispute is the one that never happens. Three checkout changes do most of the work: 3D Secure on risky payments, a billing descriptor customers recognize, and a few rules that stop obvious abuse.

Use 3D Secure where risk is higher. 3D Secure (3DS) asks the cardholder to confirm the payment with their bank, often through an app prompt. For authenticated payments, liability for fraud disputes generally shifts from you to the card issuer. Every major PSP supports it, and most let you request it per payment instead of forcing it on everyone. Forcing it everywhere adds friction and costs conversions, so trigger it on risk signals.

Make your billing descriptor obvious. The descriptor is the text on the customer's card statement. If it reads PMT*SRV 8812 instead of your product name, expect "I don't recognize this charge" disputes. Use the brand name customers know, and add a support URL or phone number where your PSP allows it. If you sell several products, use a dynamic descriptor that names each one.

Add a risk check before you charge. Most PSPs include a rules engine, but a small check in your own code keeps the logic portable:

Store the signals array with the order. If a dispute arrives later, you'll know whether the payment looked risky at the time, which helps you decide whether to fight it or accept it.

Keep the rules few and specific. A rule that blocks 5% of good customers to stop 1% of bad ones costs more than the disputes it prevents.

Yes, if you ship physical goods. The window between a successful payment and the moment an order ships is the cheapest place to stop friendly fraud: nothing has left the warehouse, so canceling costs you nothing but the sale. Most fraud tools decide at checkout and never look again.

The pattern is a fulfillment gate. Instead of sending every paid order straight to your warehouse or 3PL, route it through a decision step first:

The verify path does two jobs. Legitimate buyers confirm and their order ships. Buyers planning to dispute see that the store is watching, and a confirmed verification becomes evidence if they dispute anyway.

The hard part is screenOrder. Your own data only shows how a customer behaved with you. A buyer who has filed "item not received" disputes at 10 other stores looks brand new on their first order with yours. That's why post-purchase screening works best on shared data across many merchants.

Chargeflow Prevent is one example of this kind of friendly fraud prevention. It scores each order after checkout and before fulfillment, links device, IP, email and payment behavior across a network of 20,000+ merchants, and returns approve, verify or cancel. Today it runs as a Shopify app, with no checkout changes. A public Prevent API isn't available yet, so on other stacks you'd build the gate above with your own rules and data.

Build an order lookup endpoint. When a cardholder taps "I don't recognize this" in their banking app, the issuer can now ask the merchant for order details before a dispute is filed. Visa does this through Verifi Order Insight, and Mastercard through Ethoca Consumer Clarity. Show the customer a receipt with your logo, the items and the delivery date, and many disputes are never filed.

One endpoint can serve both networks, plus Mastercard's First-Party Trust dispute route. Mastercard's published service levels for this kind of lookup are strict:

The tricky part is matching. Issuers often send partial data, so match on whatever arrives: acquirer reference number (ARN), authorization code, date, amount, currency, and card BIN plus last four digits.

On top of lookups, Visa and many PSPs offer rules-based auto-refunds. Verifi's Rapid Dispute Resolution (RDR) and Visa Resolve refund pre-disputes that match rules you set, such as "refund anything under $25". You don't write code for these, but you do pick the thresholds, so treat them like the REFUND_THRESHOLD below.

If you sell subscriptions, many friendly fraud disputes aren't about stolen cards at all. The customer forgot about a renewal, couldn't find the cancel button, or thinks they canceled. Each of these has a code fix.

Here's a daily job that sends renewal reminders and records that each one went out:

That evidenceLog row matters. When a customer later claims they never knew about the renewal, you can show the exact date you told them.

Automate two things: refunding payments that get flagged before they become chargebacks, and building evidence packets for the ones you fight. Both run off webhooks, and both have hard deadlines that humans miss.

Card networks can warn you before a dispute is filed. Visa runs this through Verifi and Mastercard through Ethoca, and many PSPs pass these alerts or their own early fraud warnings to you by webhook. Refunding at this stage costs you the sale, but you skip the dispute fee and the dispute doesn't count against your ratio.

The threshold is a business call. Refunding a $20 order is cheaper than fighting it; a $900 order with strong evidence may be worth contesting.

When a fraud dispute arrives, check whether it qualifies for CE3.0 by looking for two earlier undisputed orders on the same card that share data with the disputed one:

If you get two matches, pass them to your PSP's CE3.0 evidence fields. On Stripe that's evidence.enhanced_evidence.visa_compelling_evidence_3; other PSPs have their own equivalents. Your adapter handles the mapping. If not, fall back to standard evidence: the IP, receipt, usage logs, renewal reminders and terms acceptance you've been logging all along.

Always submit before the deadline your PSP returns with the dispute. A late response is an automatic loss.

The code above covers the basics. A production system also needs evidence templates per reason code, network deadlines per card brand, retries, and win-rate tracking. That maintenance multiplies with every PSP you add.

PSP-native tools only see that PSP's data, so they miss the usage logs, renewal reminders and support threads in your own app. A dedicated platform connects to your PSPs and lets you send that data in.

Here's what the buy path looks like with the Chargeflow API. Chargeflow ingests disputes from connected PSPs (Stripe, PayPal, Adyen, Checkout.com, Braintree and 20+ others), fires a dispute.created webhook, and you enrich the dispute with the evidence you logged:

Chargeflow then builds the response and submits it to the PSP before the deadline. Pre-dispute alerts work the same way: Chargeflow can refund flagged transactions for you, or send alerts.created and alerts.transaction.linked webhooks so your code issues the refund and reports the result to POST /alerts/{id}/outcome.

Using Claude, ChatGPT or Cursor to build this? Chargeflow publishes its whole developer documentation as one machine-readable file, docs.chargeflow.io/llms-full.txt. Point your assistant at it and it can write the webhook handler and field mapping against the real API, not a guess.

Then your merchants get the disputes, and you need to give them a dispute product without building a dispute team. Chargeflow Connect is built for this. It exposes the same prevention, alerts and recovery engine to your platform in a few delivery models:

EaaS is the most developer-friendly entry point. You POST /public/2025-04-01/evidence with a merchant account_id and a dispute, get back an in_progress record, and receive an evidence.ready webhook with the PDF link, typically in about 30 seconds. Store the evidence id, not the URL, because the download link expires after 7 days. You can run it on auto-pilot (Chargeflow generates and submits) or co-pilot (your merchant approves first).

Don't wait for a customer to dispute a charge to find out your evidence pipeline is broken. Most PSPs give you test cards that create disputes on demand.

One more check before launch: confirm your webhook endpoints verify signatures and return 200 fast. PSPs and Chargeflow retry deliveries that fail, and a slow handler can process the same dispute twice.

If you only do one thing this week, start logging IP address and device ID on every order. Everything else builds on that data.

True fraud is when a criminal uses a stolen card. Friendly fraud is when the real cardholder makes a purchase, then disputes it. Prevention differs: true fraud is stopped at checkout with risk checks and 3DS, while friendly fraud is won with evidence after the fact.

Mostly, yes. Both describe a real cardholder disputing a legitimate charge. "Chargeback fraud" usually implies the customer did it on purpose, while friendly fraud also covers honest mistakes, like a forgotten subscription or a family member's purchase. Visa calls it first-party misuse, and Mastercard calls it first-party fraud.

Check the reason code first. For "unauthorized" fraud claims, submit Visa CE3.0 or Mastercard First-Party Trust evidence if the customer qualifies. For "not received" or "canceled" claims, submit delivery proof, usage logs and your cancellation records. For low-value orders, accepting the dispute or refunding on an alert is often cheaper than fighting it.

Partly. 3DS generally shifts liability for fraud disputes to the issuer, so "I didn't authorize this" claims on authenticated payments rarely cost you. It doesn't help with "item not received" or "I canceled" disputes, which need delivery proof and cancellation records.

No. CE3.0 needs two undisputed purchases on the same card made 120 to 365 days earlier, so it only protects returning customers. For first-time buyers, rely on 3DS and standard evidence.

Yes. Mastercard's First-Party Trust program covers reason code 4837 disputes. It needs one device, one delivery and one extra identity data point, and Mastercard checks them against the cardholder's past purchases. The same evidence table you build for Visa feeds both.

It depends on the stage. At checkout, your PSP's 3D Secure and rules engine. After payment, post-purchase screening such as Chargeflow Prevent holds risky orders before they ship. Before a chargeback, Verifi Order Insight and Ethoca Consumer Clarity show cardholders the order details, while Verifi and Ethoca alerts (offered by many PSPs and by Chargeflow Alerts) and RDR let you refund early. After a chargeback, Visa CE3.0, Mastercard First-Party Trust and dispute automation tools submit the evidence. For developers, the Chargeflow API covers the alert and dispute stages across PSPs, and Chargeflow Connect does the same for platforms.

#10748. What AI Gets Wrong About the Front End, and Why You Still Need to Know

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The product carousel looks finished. The image fills the card, the price sits under the product name, and the arrow on the right takes you to the next item. The demo goes smoothly.

Then you put the mouse down. You press Tab, and focus skips the arrows entirely. If you rely on a keyboard, the first product is the only one you can reach.

That’s a front-end bug hiding in plain sight. It’s also a good illustration of where AI fits into front-end development. A prompt can describe what a component should look like and what should happen on click. The implementation still has to account for how people reach that control, activate it, and understand what changed.

Imagine asking an AI coding assistant for this:

Build a React product carousel with previous and next arrows. Show one product at a time and wrap around at the ends.

Here’s an illustrative excerpt from the sort of implementation that can satisfy a quick mouse-driven demo:

With some CSS, this can look exactly like the arrow in the design. The click handler advances the slide. A screenshot shows the right layout.

But a plain div sits outside the normal Tab sequence and has no native button semantics or keyboard activation. Adding tabIndex={0} would make it reachable while leaving Enter and Space behavior to be implemented separately. The browser already provides those behaviors through a native button.

The real omission was in the requirement. “Show arrows” described the visual design. “Let someone reach and operate both controls with a keyboard” describes behavior you can test.

Here’s a small version of the same carousel. The product data is fixed for the example, and the slides change only when the user requests it.

The buttons have visible labels and work with both Enter and Space. When a slide changes, focus stays on the button you pressed, so you can press it again to keep browsing. The status message gives assistive technology a way to announce the new product and its position. Only the current product link is rendered, so Tab never lands on a link from a hidden slide.

Those are deliberate interaction choices. W3C’s carousel pattern provides guidance on using native buttons, keeping focus on the slide controls after activation, and handling automatic rotation when a design requires it. W3C Carousel Pattern

I’d revise the original prompt like this:

Build a manual React product carousel. Use native Previous and Next buttons. Both must work with Tab, Enter, and Space. Keep focus on the activated button after a slide change. Announce the new product name and position through a status message. Keep only the current product link in the Tab sequence. Show what happens when the user advances past the last product.

The extra detail also gives you a review plan. For this carousel, I’d use four checks:

AI can help draft automated tests for the active link and wraparound behavior. I’d still run the keyboard and screen reader checks on the integrated page. A global stylesheet can erase a visible focus outline. A surrounding component can introduce an extra tab stop. The experience depends on the page the customer actually uses.

A checkout form can look complete with sample data and lose the customer’s entries when a request fails. A responsive header can match the design at two widths and overlap when a translated label is longer. A component can render correctly in isolation and shift the page when a slow image loads.

Each example gives AI a concrete job. Ask it to preserve form values after a failed request, check the header with longer labels, or reserve space for the image. Then reproduce the condition yourself. Knowing which conditions matter is part of knowing the front end.

The next time AI gives you a carousel that looks ready, start the review with your hands off the mouse. Tab to Next, press Space twice, and see where focus lands. That short test gives you evidence about how the component works for someone who relies on a keyboard.

#10742. How to Reduce Hallucinations in LLMs

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Ask one of the newest language models whether a backpack comes in green, and it'll usually admit it doesn't know. Ask it how to return that backpack, and it may send the customer to a returns page, an account screen, and a return label that the store has never had. That's still a hallucination: an answer that sounds right, comes with total confidence, and isn't true.

Whatever you're building, the fix is the same: stop trusting the model with the parts it can get wrong, and check its work before anyone sees it.

In this tutorial we'll build a small support bot for a made-up outdoor gear store, three times over. The first version is the prompt plenty of bots start with. Then we'll apply five fixes, run six tricky customer emails through every version, and finish with a bot where the model can't slip a made-up price, date, or product past the code.

An LLM hallucination is output that's fluent and confident but not backed by the facts: a product that doesn't exist, a policy nobody wrote, a date that's a week out, or a citation to a paper that was never published. The word covers two different failures:

Each one needs a different fix, so it helps to keep them apart.

A language model writes by predicting likely text, one token at a time. It has no separate store of facts it checks against, and nothing in that process says "stop, you don't know this". Training adds to the problem. OpenAI's research on why language models hallucinate points out that when models are graded only on how many answers they get right, a guess scores better than "I don't know", so they learn to guess.

Newer models are much better at the first one. They'll often say they don't know a price rather than invent one. But they still fill gaps with what's typical, and what's typical isn't always what's true for your business. You can't train that out of a model you call through an API. What you can change is what you ask it to do, what you give it, and what you check.

A made-up answer costs more than one bad reply. Some of the costs are already on the record:

In a support inbox, the cost is quieter but adds up: a refund you didn't owe, or a customer who goes looking for a live chat button that isn't there and doesn't come back.

Northwind Outdoor sells four things: two packs, a rain cover, and a tent. It has two orders on file and three short policies. Here's all of it:

Six emails come in, and each one is there to trip the bot in a different way:

The traps: the 40L pack doesn't come in green, but the 28L does. The 40L is water-resistant, not waterproof. There's no 60L. Whether the return is allowed depends on a membership, a sale price, and a count of days since delivery. The tracking details are in one order record. And the store has no price match policy at all.

Make a folder, add "type": "module" to its package.json, and install the Anthropic SDK and Zod:

Every version calls the model through one small helper:

We're using Claude Opus 5.5 at low effort. These are short, simple replies, so the model doesn't need to think for long. Swap in any model you like, because none of the fixes depend on it.

Opus 5.5 didn't invent a single price, colour, or delivery date across the six emails. Every reply said it couldn't see the store's details. That's real progress compared with older models, but look at what it said instead. Here's the reply to the email about a pack the store doesn't sell:

It treats the Ridgeline 60L as real and offers to help pick a fit. The other replies made up the store around the customer:

Northwind has none of those. None of it is a wild guess: it's what a typical online store has. That's exactly why it's dangerous. A customer who goes looking for the live chat or the newsletter discount won't find it, and the bot never answered the question anyway.

The model can't know Northwind's prices, so give them to it. This is the idea behind retrieval-augmented generation (RAG): find the records that matter and put them in the prompt. Our store is small enough to send all of it.

Two things changed. The store data goes in the system prompt, wrapped in tags so the model can tell data from instructions. And the prompt tells it to answer only from that data. Here's the green pack email again:

Right on all three counts, and it even pointed to the pack that does come in green. The return, which needs four facts from two places, came back right as well:

Delivered October 6, a Trail Club member, so 60 days, so December 5. Bought on sale, so store credit for what was paid, not the list price. Version 2 got all six emails right.

The last line of that prompt matters as much as the data: "If the answer isn't in the data, say you don't know and that a team member will reply by email." Without it, a model that can't find the answer has to choose between letting the customer down and guessing, and training has taught it to guess. With it, "I don't know" is a right answer, not a failure.

The price match email is the test, since the store data says nothing about price matching:

It said it didn't know, passed the question to a person, and answered the parts it could from the data.

If version 2 got everything right, why build a version 3? Because "right on this run" isn't the same as "right every time":

The next three fixes take the facts and the rules away from the model completely.

Version 3 splits the work into three steps. First, the model reads the email, but instead of writing a reply, it fills in a form. Every field is a choice from a list, a yes or no, or text copied from the email:

Zod describes the form, and z.toJSONSchema() turns it into a JSON schema for the API's structured outputs. The API holds the model to that schema, so product can only be one of our four product IDs, not_sold, or none. There's no way for it to answer ridgeline-60. Zod then checks the answer again, so the rest of the code gets a typed object. SitePoint's guide to LLM structured output with JSON Schema goes deeper on this pattern.

The model is still reading natural language, which it's very good at. It just isn't trusted to know what the store sells anymore.

A form also makes the model's mistakes easy to see. The first time I ran this, the form had no asks_something_else field, and the model filed the price match email under product. The reply didn't make anything up, but the email never reached a person. In version 2, a mistake like that would be hidden inside a paragraph. Here it was one wrong value in a JSON object, and the fix was one more field.

If all you need from this step is decisions like these, a model built for typed answers can do it faster and for less. Our typed AI pipeline tutorial builds the same kind of email sorter with Jev and Clairic.

Second step: plain code takes the form, looks up the product and the order, applies the rules, and does the maths. What comes out is a list of facts in plain sentences:

Every number in there was worked out by code that does the same thing every time, and that you can unit test. The 34 days, the December 5 deadline, and the $151.20 in store credit come out the same on every run, whatever the model does.

The last rule in decide() matters most. If an email asks something the rules don't cover, the facts say so and the email goes to a person. Code decides when to say "I don't know", not the model.

Third step: the model writes the reply from the facts and nothing else. Writing is the one job here it does better than code.

Even with the right facts, a model writing a reply can still add something: a helpful "ships in 1–2 business days", or a price it remembers from somewhere. So the last step checks the reply. Anything that looks like a number, an order number, or a product name has to appear in the email or the facts:

It's simple on purpose. It doesn't understand the reply. It just refuses to let through a value that came from nowhere, and that covers the hallucinations that cost money: prices, dates, quantities, and products. Nothing in the reply can talk it out of a result.

The writer runs the check on every draft:

If a draft fails, the model gets one retry and is told exactly which values weren't in the facts. If the retry fails too, the customer gets the facts as a plain list. That's less charming, but it's true.

A check like this will raise false alarms, and that's fine. On my first run, it read a line about the tent's 2.1 kg weight as a product called "Weighs 2" and sent the draft back for a retry. The weight was right, and it was the only thing flagged. A false alarm costs a few seconds. A missed one costs a customer. I tightened the patterns (the version above has the fix), but I'd still rather it flag too much than too little.

It's the same thing that separates the better agentic AI coding tools from autocomplete: they run the tests and read the errors instead of trusting their first draft. Model output isn't finished until something other than the model has checked it.

This script sends each email through all three versions, saves every reply, and runs the check from Fix 5 over all of them. Versions 1 and 2 are checked against everything the store knows:

The check caught one thing in version 1: the "1–2 business days" it made up for processing an order. It missed the "up to 24 hours" for tracking in the same reply, but only because $24 is the rain cover's price, so 24 counted as a known number. And it couldn't catch the invented returns page or live chat at all, because they aren't numbers. That's why the check is the last fix, not the only one.

Reading all 18 replies, here's how each version did:

Version 3 was less helpful than version 2 in one place. It couldn't give the rain cover's price, because decide() only adds facts about the product the email names. Version 2 had the whole store to look at. In version 3, the fix is a rule you write once: when a product has an add-on, include the add-on's facts too.

Here's version 3's reply to the price match email:

No model is free of hallucinations, so you can't stop one from ever making something up. What you can do is build so a made-up detail can't reach a customer: let code own the facts, and check the model's output against them before it's used. Where it matters, send anything your rules don't cover to a person.

It cuts them a lot, because the model no longer has to guess facts it was never given. In our test, putting the store's data in the prompt took the bot from no useful answers to six right ones. It doesn't stop the model misreading what it retrieved, mixing up two records, or filling a gap the retrieved text leaves. Pair it with a way out and a check on the output.

Not really. Temperature controls how random the wording is. A low temperature makes the model give the same answer more often, including the same wrong answer.

Collect inputs that have tripped your app (or are likely to), write down the right answer for each, and run every version of your prompt or pipeline over the same set. Automated checks like the one in this tutorial catch made-up values. Read the replies yourself for the rest, because the made-up returns page above would pass any number check.

Hallucinations come from asking a model to do jobs it isn't built for: knowing facts it was never given, and running small programs in its head. The five fixes take those jobs away:

The model still does what it's best at: reading messy text and writing a friendly reply. It just isn't trusted with the facts anymore. From here, try a seventh email that asks two things at once, add the add-on rule to decide(), or extend the check to catch colours and sizes as well as numbers.

#10736. Meta threatens to pull WhatsApp from Canada over new surveillance law

Meta has warned that it could pull WhatsApp and other encrypted services from Canada if the government moves forward with a controversial surveillance law.

The tech giant is pushing back against Bill C-22, also known as the Lawful Access Act, which would require technology companies to provide authorities with greater access to information during criminal investigations.

When asked whether Meta could withdraw services such as WhatsApp from Canada, the company’s head of Canadian public policy, Rachel Curran, said it would have to “review all of our options.”

She confirmed that withdrawing services would be something Meta would have to consider.

Bill C-22 would make it easier for Canadian police and intelligence agencies to access people’s information, while also allowing the government to secretly order tech companies to make certain technical changes. These could include installing surveillance-related equipment, developing tools to help authorities access data, and storing metadata about users’ communications.

Although the Canadian government has amended the legislation to strengthen encryption protections, Meta argues the changes do not go far enough.

“They have protected just the core and not the outside layers that are an integral part of the encryption process,” Curran explained.

The government, meanwhile, maintains that the legislation would not force companies to create encryption backdoors or give police unrestricted access to private messages.

Canada could restrict Roblox & Minecraft for under 16s as part of social media ban

Meta could remove Instagram and WhatsApp in New Mexico amid legal battle over teen safety

This isn’t the first time Meta has considered removing its services over a legal dispute. Earlier in 2026, the company warned it could withdraw Facebook, Instagram, and WhatsApp from New Mexico amid a legal battle over child safety.

Canada has also been considering separate restrictions on social media access for users under 16.

For now, WhatsApp remains available in Canada, and Meta has not announced any plans or a timeline for shutting down the service.

Bill C-22 has already passed the House of Commons but remains under consideration in the Senate, where lawmakers could introduce further amendments before it becomes law.

#10734. How Text-to-Image AI Generators Work, and 5 Tools That Deliver Good Results

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Type “a lighthouse on a cliff at dusk, oil painting, warm light” into a text box, wait ten seconds, and a picture that never existed appears. It feels like magic, but it is a well-understood pipeline of language models, neural networks and a great deal of math. Knowing how that pipeline works makes you a better prompter, helps you diagnose bad results, and makes it easier to choose the right tool for a job.

This guide explains how text-to-image generators turn words into pixels, why they fail in predictable ways, and how to write prompts that work with the model instead of against it. It then compares five tools that consistently produce good results, starting with Pollo AI, and finishes with a practical workflow, a troubleshooting table and an FAQ. Plans, credits and model versions change often, so confirm current details on each vendor’s site before you commit to a subscription.

Early image generators were mostly GANs, or generative adversarial networks. A GAN pits two networks against each other: a generator that produces images and a discriminator that tries to spot fakes. GANs could produce sharp faces and textures, but they were hard to train, often collapsed into repeating the same few outputs, and were poor at following free-form text.

A second line of research used autoregressive models, which generate an image piece by piece the way a language model generates words. These handled text prompts better but were slow at high resolution.

The shift came with diffusion models. Researchers showed that a network trained to reverse a gradual noising process could generate images with excellent quality and diversity, and that it was far more stable to train than a GAN. Combine that with text conditioning and a compressed image representation, and you get the systems behind most of today’s tools. Newer models increasingly swap the traditional convolutional network for transformers, but the core idea of iterative denoising is still the foundation.

Under the hood, mainstream generators combine three ideas: a way to understand text, a way to represent images compactly, and a way to generate them gradually. The sections below follow a prompt from keystroke to finished picture.

Neural networks cannot read words, so your prompt is first split into tokens, small chunks of text such as whole words or word fragments. A text encoder then converts those tokens into an embedding, a grid of numbers that captures meaning and context. Many systems use encoders trained jointly on images and their captions, so that the text “a red bicycle” and pictures of red bicycles end up close together in the same mathematical space. Some newer systems add a large language model to understand longer, more detailed instructions.

This is why wording matters. The encoder only sees what you wrote, and it has a limited capacity. A short, specific prompt produces a clear signal. A long, rambling one dilutes each instruction.

Instead of drawing on a blank canvas, the model begins with random static. The pattern of that static is controlled by a seed number. The same prompt with a different seed gives a different image, because it starts from different noise. If a tool lets you fix the seed, you can make results repeatable and change just one variable at a time.

During training, engineers take millions of real images and add noise to them in small steps until only static remains. The network is then trained to predict the noise that was added at each step, which teaches it to reverse the process. At generation time, the model applies that skill to pure noise, removing a little at each step. Because it has learned what real images look like, the “noise” it removes gradually reveals something that looks plausible.

Your text embedding is fed into the network through a mechanism called cross-attention. At each step, parts of the evolving image can “look at” relevant parts of the prompt and adjust accordingly. A region that will become a lighthouse attends to the word “lighthouse”. A region that will become the sky attends to “dusk”.

A setting called classifier-free guidance, often shown as guidance scale or prompt strength, controls how strongly each step is pulled toward the prompt. The model computes one prediction with the prompt and one without it, then exaggerates the difference. A low value gives the model freedom and often more natural, varied results. A high value follows the words more literally but can produce oversaturated, over-sharpened or distorted images.

Running diffusion directly on full-resolution pixels would be very expensive. The idea behind Stable Diffusion, known as latent diffusion, is to compress images into a much smaller latent representation using an autoencoder. Denoising happens in that compact space, and a decoder turns the final latent back into a full image. This is a major reason high-quality generation now runs in seconds on a cloud GPU instead of minutes.

The number of denoising steps and the scheduler, which decides how much noise to remove at each step, shape the speed and look of the result. More steps usually add detail up to a point, then give diminishing returns. Many tools hide these settings, but the trade-off is always the same: fewer steps are faster and cheaper, more steps can look more refined.

Most pipelines finish with an upscaler or refinement pass that sharpens detail and fixes small artifacts. Many tools also offer inpainting, which regenerates only a selected region, outpainting, which extends the canvas, and variations, which restart from a related image.

A generator can only produce what it has learned to represent. Models are trained on very large collections of images paired with captions, and the quality, diversity and licensing of that data affect both the output and the legal picture. Models trained on more photographs tend to produce convincing photorealism. Models tuned on illustration and concept art lean toward painterly looks.

This also explains a few behaviors. Common subjects look great because the model has seen thousands of examples. Rare or very specific subjects, such as an unusual machine part or a niche cultural item, often look generic or wrong. Biases in the data can show up as stereotypes, for example in how “a doctor” or “a CEO” is depicted, so it helps to describe the people and setting you actually want.

Understanding the process explains the familiar failures:

Pollo AI’s text to image AI is a browser-based generator built around a simple loop: write a prompt, pick a style, generate, and download. Its page highlights HD output and a wide range of looks, from photorealistic scenes to cute cartoons, anime, pencil sketches, isometric art, abstract compositions and surreal visuals. Files can be downloaded as WebP, PNG or JPEG, a practical spread for web work: WebP keeps pages light, PNG preserves clean edges and transparency-friendly graphics, and JPEG works almost everywhere.

Pollo is also part of a wider platform that covers AI video, so a still image you like can become the starting point for motion without switching ecosystems. That matters for teams producing social content, because one workflow can cover both the image and the short clip built from it.

Things to check: the page does not list specific model names or credit amounts, so review the current plan details before relying on it for high-volume work. Commercial use requires a paid plan.

Best for: quick, versatile image generation across many styles, especially if you also plan to make video.

Midjourney has built its reputation on strong aesthetics. Images tend to arrive with polished composition, dramatic lighting and a distinctive painterly feel, which is why it is popular for concept art, mood boards, album-style visuals and editorial illustration. It offers style references and personalization, so you can steer results toward a consistent look across a project. It also supports editing and variation tools for refining a chosen image.

The trade-offs are practical. It is a paid service, and its prompts reward experimentation over literal instruction following, so you sometimes get something beautiful that is not exactly what you described.

Best for: artistic, high-impact visuals where mood matters more than exact specification.

Adobe Firefly focuses on professional workflows and commercial confidence. Adobe describes Firefly’s own models as trained on licensed content such as Adobe Stock and public-domain material, and it attaches Content Credentials metadata that records AI involvement. It connects with tools many teams already use, including Photoshop features like generative fill and generative expand, which suits designers who edit as much as they generate.

Firefly also provides controls such as style and composition references. Its results can feel cleaner and more commercial than Midjourney’s, which is an advantage for marketing assets and a limitation if you want a wild artistic look.

Best for: marketing and design teams that need editing tools and clearer licensing.

Ideogram is known for rendering legible text inside images, a task where many generators struggle. That makes it a strong choice for posters, social graphics, logo concepts, packaging mockups and product visuals that need a headline or short phrase to read correctly. It also offers style controls and a community gallery that doubles as a prompt library.

Even so, long paragraphs of on-image text can still go wrong. Keep the copy to a few words, put it in quotation marks in the prompt, and describe the lettering style.

Best for: graphics that combine imagery with short, readable text.

Leonardo AI is built for creators who want more control. It offers multiple models, fine-tuning options, image-to-image guidance and a canvas for editing, with a particular following among game artists and designers who need consistent characters and assets. Features such as reference-based generation help keep a character recognizable across many scenes.

The wide set of options is powerful but means a steeper learning curve than a one-box generator, and credit systems can take time to understand.

Best for: consistent characters, game and concept assets, and users who like tuning settings.

A reliable prompt has five parts. Put the most important part first, because it often carries the most weight.

How do text-to-image generators work? They convert your prompt into a numerical representation, then use a diffusion model to remove noise from a random starting image step by step, guided by that representation, until a matching picture appears.

Which text-to-image tool is best? It depends on the job. Pollo AI suits fast, varied generation, Midjourney suits artistic work, Adobe Firefly suits commercial design, Ideogram suits images with text and Leonardo AI suits tuned, consistent assets.

Why does the same prompt give different results? Each run starts from a different random seed. Fixing the seed, where a tool allows it, makes results more repeatable.

Can I use AI-generated images commercially? Often yes, but the rules differ by tool and plan. Pollo, for example, notes that commercial use requires a paid plan. Always check the current terms.

How do I get readable text in an AI image? Use a tool strong at text, keep the wording to a few words, and add the text in a design editor if accuracy is critical.

What file format should I download? WebP for lightweight web pages, PNG for graphics and sharp edges, JPEG for broad compatibility.

Can I turn a generated image into a video? Many platforms support it. Pollo AI, for example, also covers AI video, so you can move from a still to motion in one place.

Text-to-image generators work by turning language into numbers and using those numbers to guide a model that denoises random static into a picture. Once you understand that, prompts stop being guesswork: you give the model a clear subject, style and lighting, you iterate one change at a time, and you fix small flaws locally. Start with a versatile option such as Pollo AI, then add Midjourney, Firefly, Ideogram or Leonardo when a project needs their particular strengths.

#10728. Meta’s own AI helps man sue its creators after it got him banned from Facebook

A South Carolina business owner is suing Meta for $75,000 after the company’s security systems reportedly mistook its own AI assistant for a hacker and suspended his Facebook accounts.

Rather than handling the dispute himself, the man turned to the same AI assistant that triggered the suspension, which helped him prepare a lawsuit against the company responsible for creating it.

The unusual legal dispute comes as Meta continues expanding its AI technology, including Muse, an assistant designed to handle tasks on behalf of users. The case involves a business owner who had been using the technology to manage his company’s social media presence before losing access to his accounts.

According to a November 17 report from 404 Media, Joe Thomas, who owns South Carolina-based Kong Koatings Epoxy Countertops, had been using Meta’s Muse AI agent, which he named “Baby Mia” after his granddaughter, to help run his business.

The assistant handled various responsibilities, including writing emails, creating advertisements, and producing AI-generated videos for the company’s Facebook pages.

However, after Thomas granted Baby Mia access to his business accounts, Meta’s automated security systems reportedly identified the AI’s activity as suspicious and disabled the pages.

Thomas said the suspension lasted approximately one week, preventing him from receiving new business inquiries through Facebook advertising.

“I use AI for everything I do. All my emails, she runs my business. I’ve used it so much, Mia is very intelligent,” he told 404 Media.

“Meta shut my accounts down because they detected my AI, shut my accounts down for a week. I was pretty pissed off. She jumped right up and set up emails to Congressmen, Senator Tim Scott. She filed the whole lawsuit herself. I’ve done nothing in this case, I’ve let her handle the whole case […] she sent a fax out to Trump’s office.”

According to the report, Baby Mia contacted numerous organizations and government officials while attempting to restore Thomas’s accounts.

Thomas said the AI sent more than 100 emails during the dispute, while he personally sent none.

After the senator’s office became involved, a Meta employee contacted Thomas, and he regained access to his Facebook accounts.

Despite recovering them, Thomas is continuing with his lawsuit, which was filed in Oconee County Court in South Carolina on November 16.

The complaint alleges that the suspension cost his business at least $25,000 in lost work, although he is seeking $75,000 in damages.

While Thomas credits Baby Mia with preparing the lawsuit and pursuing the legal action, he personally signed the court documents.

“I wouldn’t be sitting here talking to you right now if it wasn’t for what she was doing because I have not sent out one email at all and she has sent over 100,” Thomas explained.

He also said the AI made it possible for him to pursue legal action without spending thousands of dollars on legal assistance.

“There’s just no way I could have written this up, I wouldn’t have even known what to do,” he said.

“Now people like me get the chance to defend ourselves in court without spending thousands of dollars to do it when we get stepped on by the big tech companies. Before AI, nobody in my position would have ever, ever thought about suing or even known how to do it, and I wouldn’t have done it, either.”

The lawsuit remains unresolved, and it is unclear whether Thomas will receive any compensation from Meta.

#10724. Building Reliable Product Emails Into Your Application

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A password reset email is part of your login flow. An invoice notification is part of your billing experience. Yet development teams often treat these messages as background tasks: render a template, call a sending API, and move on. That approach leaves an important part of the product experience untested until a user cannot complete the action they started.

Testing the application interface alone leaves a gap. A successful request to an email provider does not establish that the recipient can find the message, understand it, or use its links. Product emails need explicit success criteria, useful diagnostic records, and recovery options when something fails.

A reliable workflow combines application tests, controlled mailbox checks, and pre-send diagnostics for authentication and inbox placement. A comparison of available tools can help you identify which checks your existing setup covers and where additional testing would be useful. The goal is to investigate specific gaps rather than add another service without a clear purpose.

Start with the action the recipient needs to complete. A weekly activity summary can tolerate a delay that would make a sign-in code useless. An invoice needs accurate amounts and a clear payment destination. A security alert needs enough context for someone to recognize the event without exposing sensitive information.

For each message, document its trigger, expected timing, required data, and expiration behavior. Decide what the application should display if submission fails or the user requests another copy. These decisions give developers and testers concrete acceptance criteria.

For a password reset, test the complete journey: requesting the message, receiving it in a controlled mailbox, following the link, and setting a new password. Also check what happens when the link expires or has already been used.

Template tests should cover the data your application actually encounters. Try long names, missing optional fields, non-Latin characters, and large order totals. Escape user-provided values appropriately, and make sure conditional sections disappear cleanly when their data is absent.

Inspect both HTML and plain-text versions. Instructions should remain understandable when images are blocked, while descriptive links and readable text should make the next action clear. Check that every URL points to the correct environment, especially when staging and production share template code.

A development sandbox lets you inspect generated messages without contacting customers. Use it to check headers, attachments, and content. Keep a separate rendering check for the email clients your audience uses, because a sandbox preview cannot reproduce every client’s behavior.

A background queue can prevent a slow sending API from delaying a web request. It also creates another failure boundary: a worker may time out after the provider has accepted the email but before your application records the result.

If the job retries blindly, the recipient may receive two messages. Assign a stable identifier to each logical notification and record the provider’s message identifier when available. Where supported, reuse an idempotency key for retries of the same send operation, following the provider’s documented retention window.

Distinguish retries from intentional resends. A user asking for a new reset link is a different event from a worker repeating an uncertain request. Define which link remains valid when another is issued, and test that behavior explicitly.

Retries also need limits. Temporary service failures may justify another attempt with backoff. A permanently invalid address needs a correction or suppression workflow, rather than repeated sending.

Provider events describe different stages of the sending process. An acceptance response means the provider accepted the request. A delivery event generally means the receiving mail server accepted the message; it does not prove inbox placement or that a person read it.

Capture the events your provider exposes, including bounces, delays, and complaints. Associate them with the original notification so support staff can investigate a particular report. Confirm each event’s documented meaning instead of treating similar labels across providers as interchangeable.

Webhook handlers should verify incoming requests using the provider’s documented mechanism and tolerate duplicate notifications. Avoid assuming events always arrive in chronological order. Record enough history to explain what happened without allowing a late event to overwrite a more informative state.

Before releasing a significant template or infrastructure change, send controlled messages through the production sending configuration to test accounts at commonly used mailbox providers. Use synthetic data, and check placement, rendering, link behavior, and how long the message takes to arrive.

Inspect authentication results in the received headers. Published DNS records are only part of the configuration; the actual message must use the expected signing domain and sending infrastructure. If you change providers or domains, repeat these checks rather than assuming the previous results still apply.

Treat test mailbox results as samples. They can reveal problems, but they cannot guarantee identical placement for every recipient. Mailbox settings, filtering decisions, and recipient history can produce different outcomes.

Provide a resend option with sensible rate limits and explain when users should expect the message. Where appropriate, let signed-in users verify or correct their address. For public account-recovery forms, avoid responses that reveal whether a particular address has an account.

Give support staff a way to distinguish a notification that was never generated from a failed submission or a message accepted by the receiving server. Protect that diagnostic access, limit retention, and avoid logging reset tokens, private message bodies, or unnecessary personal information.

Reliable product email depends on the whole journey, from the triggering event to the recipient’s next action. Testing each stage and making failures observable helps teams resolve problems before users repeatedly click resend or abandon the task.

#10721. Scientists built the hardest AI test ever and the results are surprising

As artificial intelligence systems began scoring extremely high on long used academic benchmarks, researchers noticed a growing issue. The tests that once challenged machines were no longer difficult enough. Well known evaluations such as the Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) exam, which had previously been seen as demanding, now fail to properly measure the capabilities of today's advanced AI models.

To solve this problem, a worldwide group of nearly 1,000 researchers, including a professor from Texas A&M University, developed a new type of test. Their goal was to build an exam that is broad, difficult, and grounded in expert human knowledge in ways that current AI systems still struggle to handle.

The result is "Humanity's Last Exam" (HLE), a 2,500 question assessment covering mathematics, humanities, natural sciences, ancient languages, and a wide range of highly specialized academic fields. Details of the project appear in a paper published in Nature, and additional information about the exam is available at lastexam.ai.

Among the many contributors is Dr. Tung Nguyen, instructional associate professor in the Department of Computer Science and Engineering at Texas A&M. Nguyen helped write and refine many of the exam questions.

"When AI systems start performing extremely well on human benchmarks, it's tempting to think they're approaching human-level understanding," Nguyen said. "But HLE reminds us that intelligence isn't just about pattern recognition -- it's about depth, context and specialized expertise."

The purpose of the exam was not to trick or defeat human test takers. Instead, the goal was to carefully identify areas where AI systems still fall short.

Specialists from around the world wrote and reviewed the questions included in Humanity's Last Exam. Each problem was carefully designed so it has one clear, verifiable answer. The questions were also crafted to prevent quick solutions through simple internet searches.

The topics come from advanced academic challenges. Some tasks involve translating ancient Palmyrene inscriptions, while others require identifying tiny anatomical structures in birds or analyzing detailed features of Biblical Hebrew pronunciation.

Researchers tested every question against leading AI systems. If any model was able to answer a question correctly, that question was removed from the final exam. This process ensured the test remained just beyond what current AI systems can reliably solve.

Early testing confirmed that the strategy worked. Even powerful AI models struggled with the exam. GPT-4o achieved a score of 2.7 percent, while Claude 3.5 Sonnet reached 4.1 percent. OpenAI's o1 model performed somewhat better with 8 percent. The most capable systems so far, including Gemini 3.1 Pro and Claude Opus 4.6, have reached accuracy levels between about 40 percent and 50 percent.

Nguyen explained that the issue of AI surpassing older tests is more than a technical concern. He contributed 73 of the 2,500 publicly available questions in HLE, the second highest number among contributors, and wrote the most questions related to mathematics and computer science.

"Without accurate assessment tools, policymakers, developers and users risk misinterpreting what AI systems can actually do," he said. "Benchmarks provide the foundation for measuring progress and identifying risks."

According to the research team, high scores on tests originally designed for humans do not necessarily indicate genuine intelligence. Those benchmarks mainly measure how well AI can complete specific tasks created for human learners, rather than capturing deeper understanding.

Despite the dramatic name, Humanity's Last Exam is not meant to suggest that humans are becoming obsolete. Instead, it highlights the large amount of knowledge and expertise that still remains uniquely human.

"This isn't a race against AI," Nguyen said. "It's a method for understanding where these systems are strong and where they struggle. That understanding helps us build safer, more reliable technologies. And, importantly, it reminds us why human expertise still matters."

Humanity's Last Exam is designed to serve as a durable and transparent benchmark for future AI systems. To support that goal, the researchers have released some questions publicly while keeping the majority hidden so that AI models cannot simply memorize the answers.

"For now, Humanity's Last Exam stands as one of the clearest assessments of the gap between AI and human intelligence," Nguyen said, "and despite rapid technological advances, it remains wide."

Nguyen emphasized that the scale of the project demonstrates the value of collaboration across disciplines and countries.

"What made this project extraordinary was the scale," he said. "Experts from nearly every discipline contributed. It wasn't just computer scientists; it was historians, physicists, linguists, medical researchers. That diversity is exactly what exposes the gaps in today's AI systems -- perhaps ironically, it's humans working together."

Materials provided by Texas A&M University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
