#10611. Top 6 AI Text to Speech Tools in 2026: Features, Strengths and Limits

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Not long ago, a synthetic voice was easy to spot: flat, rushed and strangely stressed. Today, the best AI text to speech tools can read a script with natural pacing, switch accents, whisper or laugh on cue, and even speak in a voice cloned from a short recording. That makes them useful far beyond screen readers. Developers use them for product voice prompts, marketers for video voiceovers, teachers for course narration and writers for listening back to their own drafts.

This guide explains how modern text-to-speech works, what to look for when choosing a tool, and compares six options: OpenArt, ElevenLabs, Murf AI, Speechify, WellSaid Labs and Amazon Polly. Each section covers strengths, limits and the best use case. The details come from each vendor’s own pages at the time of writing, and plans, models and prices change often, so confirm current terms before you commit.

OpenArt’s AI text to speech tool lives inside a wider creative platform. You open the Audio tab, choose Create voice-over, select a model, paste your script, pick a voice, generate, listen and download. It runs in the browser with nothing to install. According to OpenArt’s page, you can switch between models for each script, including Fish Audio Drama 3, Eleven v4 and Eleven v4 Turbo.

The direction system is what makes it practical for storytelling. You add notes in square brackets, such as [whispering], [laughs] or [pause], directly in the script. OpenArt says Fish Audio Drama 3 gives close control over single words and mid-line changes of mood, while Eleven v4 reads the whole script to infer the emotion of each line and keeps the voice steady when you regenerate a line. Preset voices are labeled by accent, gender and age, and you can upload a recording of your own voice to clone it.

Because OpenArt also makes video and background music, you can build the voiceover, visuals and soundtrack in one place. It describes a Director mode for planning stories up to five minutes, and the voiceover can also be added in any video editor.

Pricing: New accounts get 40 free credits. Paid plans add monthly credits and watermark-free downloads, and commercial rights apply on eligible paid plans. Check the pricing page for current plans.

Best for: creators who want expressive voiceovers together with AI video and music.

ElevenLabs is one of the best-known names in AI voice. Its text to speech page describes a large voice library, voice design and both instant and professional voice cloning, with the latest model, Eleven v4, positioned as the most expressive. It also lists Eleven v3, Multilingual v2 and a low-latency Flash and Turbo family aimed at real-time use, and it claims support for more than 90 languages and accents, with the exact count varying by model.

For developers, there is a text to speech API with SDKs, which makes it practical for apps, games, agents and automated pipelines. The free plan includes 10,000 credits a month, and paid plans start with Starter at $6 a month, then Creator at $22 and Pro at $99, according to the vendor page, with introductory offers on the first month.

Pricing: Free plan with 10,000 credits a month. Paid plans from $6 a month as listed on the vendor page.

Best for: expressive narration, multilingual content and apps that need a voice API.

Murf AI positions itself as a studio for voiceovers. Its page lists more than 200 voices, built with permission and partnership from professional voice actors who earn royalties, and support for 35 or more languages for text to speech, with dubbing in 40 or more. The Studio editor offers pitch, speed and intonation controls and a custom pronunciation library, which helps with brand names and technical terms.

Murf also has an API. Its page describes Falcon 2 for conversational use with time-to-first-audio under 100 milliseconds, priced at $0.01 per minute, and Gen 2 for controllable voiceovers. Gen 2’s “Say it My Way” feature lets you record your own rendition so the model reflects your tone and inflections.

Pricing: Free plan with limits. Paid plan prices are on Murf’s pricing page.

Best for: marketing videos, training and presentations where you want to fine-tune delivery.

Speechify is built around reading aloud. You paste text or upload a PDF, DOCX, TXT or EPUB file, pick a voice, adjust playback speed and listen or download the audio. Its page lists more than 1,000 voices across 60 or more languages, including several regional English accents, and the tool works in the browser and in apps for iOS, Android, Mac and Windows, plus Chrome and Edge extensions.

The use cases lean toward productivity and accessibility: studying, going through emails and documents, language learning and support for people with visual impairments, dyslexia or ADHD. A free account saves your documents and syncs them across devices.

Pricing: You can start free. Paid plans are listed on Speechify’s pricing page.

Best for: students, professionals and anyone who wants to listen to documents and articles.

WellSaid Labs focuses on professional voiceover for businesses. Every voice is built from recordings of a real voice actor who consented and is paid when the voice is used, and the voices are licensed for commercial use. Its page describes hundreds of voices across languages, accents, tones and styles.

Pronunciation control is a highlight. You can highlight a word to see suggested pronunciations and save your choice to a team library that applies to every project, and set pacing, emphasis and pronunciation line by line. WellSaid also describes a Producer product that connects AI assistants to WellSaid through MCP and its API.

Pricing: Contact sales for a quote based on your voiceover volume.

Best for: corporate training, product marketing, courses and advertising where licensing and consistency matter.

Amazon Polly is Amazon Web Services’ text to speech service, and it is aimed squarely at developers. According to AWS, it supports more than 40 languages and language variants with over 100 male and female voices, and it works through APIs in your code. It supports SSML, the W3C standard markup for phrasing, emphasis and intonation, and custom lexicons that fix the pronunciation of acronyms and terms. Output formats include MP3 and OGG.

Pricing is usage-based. AWS lists a free tier for new accounts, with usage allowances that depend on the voice engine, so estimate your character volume before you decide. A short example shows how little code a basic request takes:

The sample uses the AWS SDK for JavaScript, calls the neural engine and sends SSML so that the pause and emphasis are explicit. Adjust the region, voice and engine to match what your account supports.

Pricing: Usage-based with a free tier for new accounts. See the AWS pricing page for current rates.

Best for: apps, IVR systems, notifications and any product that needs automated speech at scale.

Treat this table as a starting point. Features and limits change often, so test two or three tools with the same script.

Modern text-to-speech systems turn written words into audio through several stages.

Newer expressive systems go further by reading the whole script to infer emotion and context, and by accepting direction in the text itself, such as a bracketed note that says to whisper. That is why a single script can produce very different reads depending on the model and the instructions.

If you use a tool with SSML support, you can make a spoken code or number far clearer:

The say-as element tells the engine to read the number digit by digit, and the break and emphasis tags shape the rhythm. Supported tags and attributes differ between engines, so check the documentation for yours.

What is the best AI text to speech tool? It depends on the job. OpenArt suits voiceovers with video and music, ElevenLabs suits expressive multilingual audio, Murf AI suits studio-style control, Speechify suits listening, WellSaid Labs suits licensed corporate work and Amazon Polly suits developers.

Is AI text to speech free? Several tools offer free plans or credits, but limits are common. Examples include OpenArt’s free credits for new accounts, ElevenLabs’ free monthly credits and Murf’s limited free plan, which has no commercial licence.

Can I use AI voices commercially? Often yes on paid plans, but the rules differ by tool. Always check the current licence before publishing.

Can AI text to speech clone my voice? Some tools support it, including OpenArt and ElevenLabs, usually from a short recording of your own voice. Only clone voices you have permission to use.

What is SSML? Speech Synthesis Markup Language is a W3C standard that lets you control pauses, emphasis, pronunciation and pitch in synthetic speech. Amazon Polly is one service that supports it.

Which tool is best for developers? Amazon Polly and ElevenLabs both offer APIs, and Murf describes API options as well. Evaluate latency, languages, pricing and SDK support for your use case.

How do I make AI voices sound less robotic? Write conversationally, add pauses and emphasis, correct pronunciations and split long scripts into short sections. Choose a model that is designed for expressive reads.

AI text to speech is now good enough for real projects, but the right tool depends on what you are making. OpenArt is a strong first stop for expressive voiceovers alongside video and music, ElevenLabs offers a deep voice library and API, Murf AI and WellSaid Labs suit polished, controlled narration, Speechify turns documents into audio and Amazon Polly fits straight into developer workflows. Test two or three with your own script, check the licence and always listen to the full result before it goes live.

#10610. MIT’s new lidar chip could give self-driving cars a wider view

Lidar technology uses pulses of infrared light to calculate distances and create detailed 3D maps of the surrounding environment. This allows autonomous vehicles to detect objects in their path and respond quickly. However, conventional lidar sensors are often large and costly, and many rely on moving components that can wear out over time. These limitations make the systems difficult to use in a wider range of settings.

MIT researchers have now developed an approach that could lead to smaller, more durable lidar sensors that operate without any moving parts. Their advance centers on a new silicon-photonics chip, a type of semiconductor device that controls light instead of electrical signals.

Existing lidar systems built with silicon-photonics chips usually have a narrow field of view. As a result, they struggle to scan areas located toward the edges of a scene. Previous attempts to expand this viewing range have often introduced extra noise and reduced measurement accuracy.

The MIT team addressed those problems by creating an array of integrated antennas that greatly limits unwanted crosstalk, which occurs when neighboring antennas interfere with one another. The design allows the chip to scan across a broader field of view while producing less noise than other silicon-photonics-based methods.

The advance could support the development of more capable lidar sensors for challenging uses, including autonomous vehicle navigation, aerial mapping, and the monitoring of construction sites.

"The functionality we demonstrated in this work solves a fundamental problem for integrated optical-phased-array technology, enabling future lidar sensors that can achieve significantly higher performance than we could demonstrate previously," says Jelena Notaros, the Robert J. Shillman Career Development Associate Professor of Electrical Engineering and Computer Science (EECS) at MIT, a member of the Research Laboratory of Electronics, and senior author of a paper on this innovation.

The study also includes lead author and EECS graduate student Henry Crawford-Eng, along with EECS graduate students Andres Garcia Coleto, Benjamin M. Mazur, Daniel M. DeSantis, and Tal Sneh. The findings were published recently in Nature Communications.

Many traditional lidar systems use a large rotating unit to direct light pulses across a scene. When the light strikes nearby objects, it reflects back toward the sensor. The returning signals provide the information needed to reconstruct a detailed map of the environment.

Silicon-photonics-based lidar works differently. Rather than rotating a mechanical device, it scans a beam of light electronically in several directions with a system known as an integrated optical phased array (OPA).

At the heart of an OPA is a group of integrated antennas. Each antenna contains tiny, regularly spaced variations along its length. These features, known as corrugations, cause light from an input source to scatter upward and out of the photonic chip.

Researchers can control the direction of the outgoing beam by changing the phase of the light sent to each antenna. Adjusting these phases changes the angle at which the array releases light, making it possible to steer the beam without moving any physical components.

Placing the antennas close together creates a serious obstacle. Neighboring antennas can couple with one another, scrambling the light they produce. Engineers have traditionally prevented this interference by increasing the distance between antennas, but wider spacing creates a different set of problems.

When antennas are too far apart, the array produces several copies of the same beam at different angles. The primary beam can only be moved a limited distance before it becomes difficult to distinguish from these additional copies.

"This limits our field of view, so the autonomous vehicle now only knows what is in front of it for a certain angular range," Garcia Coleto explains.

The unwanted beam copies, called grating lobes, can confuse the sensor and generate false detections. They also consume energy that could otherwise be directed into the main beam.

To overcome this tradeoff, the MIT researchers developed antennas with reduced crosstalk that can be positioned close together without strongly coupling.

In a conventional OPA, every antenna has an identical structure and uses the same pattern of corrugations. When these matching antennas are placed close together, they interact very strongly.

The MIT team instead created a repeating set of three antennas with distinct shapes. They changed the width of the antennas as well as the size and placement of the corrugations. Because the antennas have different geometries, each one also has a different propagation coefficient, which describes how light moves through the structure.

"Because the antennas have very different propagation coefficients, when we put them close together, essentially each antenna doesn't 'see' the antenna next to it. Therefore, it won't couple with its neighbor," Garcia Coleto says.

Making Different Antennas Behave the Same Way

Reducing the coupling was only part of the challenge. Although the antennas needed different propagation coefficients, they still had to release light in the same consistent manner.

The team designed the antennas around three essential requirements.

Each antenna had to emit the same amount of light. Every antenna also needed to release its beam at the same angle when receiving the same wavelength. Finally, the angle of emission had to change evenly across the entire array as the beam was steered.

"We have this challenge where we require the antennas to have different geometries to reduce the crosstalk, but we need to simultaneously design the antennas to have the same emission characteristics. While it is possible to engineer this, it is extremely difficult because, typically, when antennas are designed with different geometries, they tend to behave differently," Crawford-Eng says.

The researchers began by developing the basic electromagnetic theory describing how radiative modes couple. They then used this theoretical framework to guide the design and computer simulation of the antennas.

Based on those calculations, the team manufactured an OPA containing the reduced-crosstalk antennas. The antennas were placed much closer together than those in a conventional system, and the completed device was then tested experimentally.

Interference Falls From About 100 Percent to 1 Percent

Under the conditions of the experiment, a typical OPA would have produced coupling of approximately 100 percent. The MIT design lowered that coupling to about 1 percent while still generating one clean and precise beam.

The system accurately steered the beam across a broad field of view without producing any grating lobes. This combination of wide scanning, low interference, and strong beam quality addresses one of the central obstacles facing integrated lidar technology.

The researchers now plan to refine the method so the system can cover an even broader viewing range. They are also studying another possible approach to wide field-of-view performance that emerged while they were developing the underlying theory.

"This work addresses a longstanding challenge in integrated optical phased arrays: simultaneously achieving both a wide field of view, which requires dense antenna spacing, and high beam quality, which requires low crosstalk between neighboring antennas. The authors solve this problem with an elegant antenna design. Their innovation is an important step forward for chip-scale, solid-state beam-steering technology," says Joyce Poon, professor of electrical and computer engineering at the University of Toronto and director of the Max Planck Institute of Microstructure Physics, who was not involved with this work.

The Semiconductor Research Corporation, the National Science Foundation, an MIT MathWorks Fellowship, the U.S. Department of War, and the MIT Rolf G. Locher Endowed Fellowship supported the research, in part.

Some of the work was carried out using MIT.nano facilities.

Materials provided by Massachusetts Institute of Technology. Original written by Adam Zewe. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10607. World’s first superconducting quantum heat engine could help unlock massive quantum computers

A newly developed superconducting quantum heat engine could deepen our understanding of thermodynamics while helping advance technologies needed for quantum computers with very large numbers of qubits.

Scientists are getting a clearer picture of how thermodynamics behaves in the quantum world, and that progress could benefit both quantum technology and our understanding of familiar thermodynamic principles. Researchers at Aalto University have now taken an important step by demonstrating the first cyclic quantum heat engine built inside a superconducting circuit.

The experiment connects two areas of physics that normally describe very different scales. Quantum mechanics explains the behavior of matter at extremely small scales, even below the size of atoms, while thermodynamics describes how heat and energy behave in much larger systems, from collections of molecules to the universe itself. Bringing the two together raises a fundamental question: what happens to familiar thermodynamic processes when quantum effects such as tunneling, entanglement, and superposition enter the picture?

A Heat Engine Built for the Quantum World

Conventional heat engines turn heat into useful work. James Watt's steam engine is one famous example, but the same basic concept remains central to modern transportation and electricity production, powering cars, ships, planes and many power plants.

The researchers have now created the world's first superconducting quantum heat engine. The extremely small device combines a transmon qubit, a resonator and a quantum refrigerator.

Operating under ultracold quantum conditions, the engine was able to use the tiny amount of available heat to repeatedly produce positive work. Achieving this kind of cyclic operation has been an important objective for researchers working on quantum heat engines. The result provides a proof of concept for superconducting heat engines that could eventually contribute to improved quantum computing technology.

The study, led by Academy Professor Mikko Möttönen, was published in Nature Communications.

Recreating an Otto Cycle Near Absolute Zero

To make the engine operate, the researchers reproduced an Otto cycle inside a superconducting circuit. The Otto cycle is a thermodynamic process also used in car engines and other conventional machines.

"In our experiment, we built a nanofabricated heat engine using superconducting circuits and operated it in a cryostat near absolute zero. At its heart is a transmon qubit, one of the basic building blocks of modern quantum technologies," says Tuomas Uusnäkki, the study's first author.

The researchers connected the transmon qubit to a quantum circuit refrigerator, allowing them to control heat flow on the quantum scale and demonstrate that this heat could be transformed into measurable work. A conventional heat engine normally relies on separate hot and cold environments. In this system, however, the same quantum refrigerator can supply both heating and cooling.

"Our quantum-circuit refrigerator can be tuned to both heat and cool the qubit on demand. Using carefully timed control pulses, we drove the engine in an Otto cycle and monitored the qubit state as the engine ran," explains Uusnäkki.

Measurements showed that heat passing through the qubit during the cycle was producing positive work.

"This is the first experimental demonstration of a cyclic quantum heat engine in superconducting circuits. Using a single controllable quantum refrigerator as both the hot and cold environment of the engine makes it simpler and more versatile," says Uusnäkki.

Toward Autonomous Quantum Computer Hardware

The researchers are now trying to improve the design and eventually develop a fully autonomous heat engine. One possible use would be reading out qubits without having to carry a microwave pulse from millikelvin temperatures all the way to room temperature.

That capability could become especially valuable as quantum computers grow. Autonomous devices integrated directly into superconducting circuits could reduce both the cost and complexity of machines containing very large numbers of qubits.

"Finland's Quantum Technology Strategy envisions a quantum computer with one thousand logical qubits by 2035, which probably means hundreds of thousands of physical qubits. Doing that with current technology requires millions of microwave cables costing thousand euros each. The cables also introduce noise into the system. Using autonomous devices instead would mostly eliminate the need for those cables," Möttönen says.

Reducing the need for those microwave connections could therefore address two challenges at once: the enormous hardware requirements of large quantum computers and the unwanted noise that cables can introduce into quantum systems.

The pioneering experiment was carried out using OtaNano, Finland's national research infrastructure for nano, micro and quantum technology. Funding came from the Research Council of Finland and the Finnish Cultural Foundation.

Materials provided by Aalto University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

Consumer SSDs and conventional hard drives lack the performance and endurance for extensive AI workloads. This has increased demand for enterprise SSDs. Form factors like U.2, U.3, E3.S, and E1.S, once rare, are now common in high-end workstations and home labs. These enterprise storage solutions provide higher capacities, better performance, and greater reliability than consumer drives.

Comprehensive reviews of enterprise and data center SSDs are therefore more important than ever. Buying a drive of this caliber is a major decision and a significant investment for organizations of all sizes. With this latest venture, Tom's Hardware will continue to help buyers make informed decisions with comprehensive reviews and performance analyses.

Reviewing enterprise and data center SSDs required us to build a workstation that could truly challenge these drives. This project would not have been possible without the support of several component makers, including Asus, Corsair, G.Skill, Icy Dock, and Noctua, who made the workstation a reality, a challenging prospect in the current market.

The Xeon 658X, a 24-core, 48-thread chip from the Granite Rapids family with a 4.9 GHz boost clock speed, powers the testing workstation. Single-core performance and total core count matter just as much when reviewing storage. Clock speed determines how many IOPS one core can push and how fast it answers a single request, while 24 cores let us spread a drive's load across eight fio jobs and later test several drives at once. The Xeon 658X ticks all those boxes.

We've entrusted the Xeon 658X's cooling to Noctua's NH-U14S DX-4677 air cooler, which supports both LGA4677 and LGA4710 sockets. The single-tower CPU air cooler delivers high-performance cooling at very low noise levels thanks to its dual NF-A15 140mm cooling fans.

With its server-grade components and robust enterprise features, the Asus Pro WS W890E-Sage SE is the ideal motherboard to pair with the Xeon 658X. Its extensive feature set includes an onboard MCIO x8 connection, split into x4/x4, that allows for evaluating two U.2 or U.3 drives. The board also provides seven PCIe 5.0 expansion slots (six at x16 and one at x8) to fully leverage the Xeon 658X’s 128 PCIe 5.0 lanes, so the SSDs and graphics cards never hit bandwidth limits.

We're still in need of support for an RTX Pro graphics card, which would be an invaluable asset for enterprise SSD reviews for real-world AI workloads, but our current test platform allows us to test the most important aspects of performance.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Despite the memory and storage shortage, G.Skill and Corsair have stepped up to provide crucial components for our enterprise SSD test bench. G.Skill sent a G5 DDR5-6400 C32 64GB (4x16GB) R-DIMM memory kit, which runs at the stock DDR5-5600 speed. The decision to keep it at the JEDEC baseline has nothing to do with the memory kit: Memory frequency does not affect performance in enterprise SSD tests.

Meanwhile, Corsair provided the speedy MP700 Pro XT 2TB, one of the best SSDs, as the test bench's primary storage. The PCIe 5.0 drive delivers sequential read and write speeds up to 14,900 MB/s and 14,500 MB/s, respectively, and keeps the workstation snappy, especially when it hammers enterprise drives all day long.

No power supply is better suited to the job than the Corsair HX1500i. It offers a massive 1,500W capacity with plenty of headroom for future upgrades, and it runs quietly and stably, two qualities that matter when you keep the test system running for countless hours. It is an 80 Plus Platinum power supply with at least 89% efficiency at full load.

Testing enterprise drives in a standard PCIe expansion slot is more convenient. For that purpose, Icy Dock has provided us with the ExpressSlot Elite MB221E5P-B and MB303E5P-B to accept one and two E1.S SSDs. Unlike other generic designs on the market, these adapters feature tool-less installation mechanisms that don't require removing them from the PCIe expansion slot, and they include 50mm fans for active cooling.

The list also included the ToughArmor MB218E5P-B, a 5.25-inch drive bay that supports up to eight E1.S SSDs for future projects exploring RAID arrays. To test E3.S drives, we use a JMT ADT-R27C adapter strapped to a Noctua NF-F12 industrialPPC-24V-3000 IP67 PWM cooling fan, which can run at 12V and still provide substantial airflow to actively cool the SSDs under evaluation.

A TGDGamer E-ATX test bench houses the hardware for the enterprise SSD system. It may not look flashy, but it does the job, and that is what matters. As an open-air test bench, it is easy to install and remove hardware, and it eliminates variables when it comes to heat generation and buildup. The test bench also supports motherboard form factors up to SSI-EEB, so the Asus Pro WS W890E-Sage SE feels right at home.

A spec sheet only tells you what a drive can do on its best day. The real world is not as pretty. The drive is mostly full, garbage collection is constantly at work, and multiple workloads are hitting it simultaneously. Our enterprise benchmark suite is built to measure the drive in service: at steady state, under load, through write bursts, and with other jobs running. Every drive gets the same automated run on the same test bench. Every result is stored with the exact parameters that produced it, so we can trace how we got there.

This can take some time. A full run takes most of a day on a 7.68TB PCIe 5.0 drive, and it writes the drive end-to-end many times over. To make life a bit easier on us, the benchmark can be run unattended, and it’s crash-resumable from the last stage. A shorter express mode handles bench checks and quick triage. When a result looks odd, express runs and variant runs, which repeat just the tests in question or change one setting, help us find the cause before we take it to the vendor.

We run the bench on a custom Debian 13 image that we build and maintain ourselves, with the suite copied in at build time. This image gets installed on the bench’s OS drive, and the kernel is pinned so every drive has the same software stack. Right now that’s Linux 7.2.6 from Debian backports, because Debian 13’s stock 6.12 kernel predates support for the Asus Pro WS W890E-Sage SE’s Intel E610 10GbE controller. The suite is also designed to be run remotely if needed, but we need a tester at the bench to switch the drives, of course.

Debian is our pick over Ubuntu for a few reasons: it has fewer background services, no Snap, and no unattended upgrades by default. We hold the kernel so nothing changes between runs. We remove AppArmor and turn off scheduled jobs that could fire mid-run, like the weekly TRIM timer and the drive-health service that polls every drive, and the suite records any that are still running. The suite also turns off NVMe autonomous power-state transitions (APST) at the start of every run and checks that the drive reports it off, so no drive drops into a low-power state during an idle gap and adds wake-up latency to the next I/O.

Every test runs in fio 3.42, using io_uring and direct I/O against the raw block device. This means no filesystem and no page cache sitting between fio and the drive; the bench’s 64GB of RAM never caches a byte of test data. We compile fio from source because Debian’s packaged version lacks SPRandom, which we’ll get to in short order.

Some enterprise storage reviews run application profiles in Oracle’s Vdbench, but we decided to go full fio. This makes for a more consistent suite with one set of latency accounting across every chart. And fio is open source and actively maintained, while Vdbench hasn’t had a new release since 2018. Vdbench profiles are built from the same parts fio uses, such as read/write ratio, block-size mix, and random or sequential access, and our mixed-workload sweeps cover the common read/write ratios at 4K and 8K, though not mixed-block-size profiles. Running Vdbench ourselves wouldn’t make our results comparable to other outlets’ anyway, since they use different hardware, preconditioning, and profile definitions.

A key element of benchmarking SSDs is making sure the test drive is at steady state, with the performance it can sustain rather than the peak you can see after a fresh erase. Our first stage is a secure erase using nvme format. If a drive refuses, the suite falls back to a plain format, then a full-drive discard, and records which one it used.

Next comes preconditioning. SPRandom, SanDisk’s open-source contribution to fio, is a faster alternative to filling the drive over and over for hours. It takes user capacity and over-provisioning (OP) as inputs, which SanDisk says lets it reach a steady-state layout in just one pass. Overprovisioning (OP) is inferred from the capacity point or given by the tester. NVMe drives don’t report their raw flash, so a 7.68TB drive like our first one, assumed to sit on 8TiB of actual NAND, works out to having about 14.5% OP.

We don’t take SPRandom’s result on faith. A steady-state gate follows, running 4K random writes at queue depth 256 (QD256) until the drive’s write speed stops moving. It uses the two tests from the Storage Networking Industry Association’s (SNIA) steady-state rule, with limits ten times tighter, applied to the last 30 minutes: split into five six-minute rounds, the round averages must sit within 2% of one another, and the trend line across the window can’t move more than 1%.

That takes a while, but that’s the point: an earlier, looser version of the gate passed our first drive while its write speed was still climbing. The whole settling curve is saved with every run, and a drive that doesn’t settle within three hours is marked as not reaching steady state, though the run continues. As a backstop, the suite repeats the four-corner 4K write after the random sweep, hours later, and flags the run if the two results differ by more than 3%.

The suite also checks its own results as it goes. If a test’s slowest moment falls below 70% of its average, that test is run again on the spot. If a point in the random sweep lands more than 10% off the curve set by its neighbors, it’s measured again before the drive is erased for the sequential tests. Both results are kept, and any re-test is noted in the review.

Following the random tests, the tool erases the drive again and writes it end to end once, with 128K sequential writes at QD64. We run it before the sequential and AI tests, so the drive is conditioned with the kind of I/O we’re about to measure. It matters because sequential writes can swing wildly on a randomly written drive: on our first test drive, the fastest result was more than three times the slowest. After a sequential fill, they held flat from QD2 through QD1024, at both 128K and 1MB.

Drives are formatted to 512-byte logical blocks when they support it, so our block size sweep can go down to 512-byte transfers, which a 4K-native namespace can’t issue. SNIA’s enterprise IOPS test uses 512-byte transfers too, and Kioxia pitches 512-byte access for its GP Series, which is aimed at GPU-initiated storage. Drives without a 512-byte format are tested as shipped, and their sub-4K results are marked unsupported. The switch happens in the same format command as the secure erase, so it adds no time.

A fast enough SSD will happily benchmark your host, or your tools, instead, and our early runs turned up three ways that could happen. The first is CPU idle states, or C-states: waking our Xeon 658X cores from their deepest idle state can take up to 170 microseconds, and at QD1 that directly impacts the results. Holding the cores awake for the whole run cut the first drive’s average 4K QD1 read latency by about a sixth.

The second is the CPU itself: a single fio job can saturate its core well short of what a PCIe 5.0 drive can manage. Split across eight jobs, the same test runs several times faster, so random tests now spread their queue depth across up to eight fio jobs. That’s also why fast cores, and enough of them, matter for this test bench.

The third is fio itself. By default, fio seeds its random generator the same way every run, so it replays the same sequence of random offsets, and each random write test rewrites the previous test’s blocks in the same order. On our first drive, that was the main reason 4K random writes came in at more than twice the sustained figure. Every random test now runs with a fresh random seed.

What we don’t change matters, too. Completions arrive by interrupt rather than polling, and the IOMMU stays in its default mode, because that’s how most applications use a drive. On our first drive, polling and IOMMU passthrough would each have shaved some latency off 4K QD1 writes.

Interrupts also set a ceiling at the other end of the scale: this bench tops out near 2.8 million 4K random read IOPS, and adding fio jobs doesn’t lift it, though polling does. So on the fastest drives, our 4K random read result can sit below the spec sheet’s, and that’s our bench’s limit, not the drive’s. Our low queue depth latencies, writes especially, run higher than a tuned host would show, but they’re closer to what you’d actually see. To show the drive’s limit at both ends, the suite repeats three tests with polled I/O at the end of the random stage: the 4K random read peak, plus 4K random reads and writes at QD1. Those results get their own charts and never mix into the main ones.

4K random read and write at QD256; 128K and 1MB sequential read and write at QD64

The headline numbers, measured on a preconditioned drive instead of fresh out of the box

4K and 8K, QD1 to QD4096, eight read/write mixes from 100% read to 100% write

Servers rarely run flat out; QD1 is what latency-bound software feels, and 8K is a common database page size

4K random read at QD1024 across eight jobs, plus 4K random read and write at QD1, all with polled I/O

What the drive can do with the host’s interrupt overhead out of the way; the results to set against a spec sheet

128K and 1MB sequential reads and writes, QD1 to QD1024

How much parallelism a drive needs to reach full speed; low queue depths are what a single restore or scan gets

Random reads and writes from 512 bytes to 1MB at QD32

Writes smaller than a drive’s mapping unit can pay a read-modify-write penalty; large blocks show its bandwidth cap

4K and 8K reads and writes at QD1, QD4, and QD16, from the median (P50) to the 99.999th percentile (P99.999)

Averages hide the stalls applications notice; at a million IOPS, the slowest 0.001% is still ten requests a second

4K 70/30 at 13 queue depths from QD1 to QD4096, plotted as a share of the drive’s own peak

Finds the load where latency starts to climb

Whether sustained writes hold a flat line or sawtooth as garbage collection struggles

4K QD1 reads for four minutes right after a heavy write burst

How far reads drop after heavy writes, and how fast they recover

Write bursts after idle gaps of 0 to 300 seconds

How much write speed a quiet spell buys back

4K QD1 log writes with a sync after each, a random 8K 70/30 mix at QD16, and a 256K sequential read scan

Log-write latency, page I/O, and analytics scans, without benchmarking a database’s RAM cache

Checkpoint-sized saves and restores modeled on 8B- and 70B-class models (105GiB over eight writers, 912GiB over 64)

Time until the slowest writer finishes, which is when a real checkpoint would be done

A fixed 4K read load, alone and during a checkpoint save

What a checkpoint does to serving traffic on the same drive

Drive temperature through the hour-long sustained write test

Measured against the drive’s own warning and critical thresholds

Some things are out of scope by design. We review drives; we don’t certify them. That rules out dual-port failover, power-loss validation, and single-root I/O virtualization (SR-IOV), which belong in a qualification lab. We’ll add tests when they prove themselves on the bench. Many of the tests in the table are old standbys and still earn their place. Others are newer and require further explanation.

For one, we chart latency against load: the 70/30 4K mix from the random sweep, at 13 queue depths from QD1 to QD4096, with throughput shown as a share of the drive’s own peak. Tail latency climbs as the load grows, and the point where it turns sharply upward (on our first drive, that was write latency) represents the practical operating ceiling, or the load you can plan a server around without giving up low latency.

We also test write-to-read recovery, which measures how a drive’s reads hold up after a heavy write burst. A few minutes of 4K random writes at QD256 leave the drive with cleanup to do, and QD1 reads are then timed over the next four minutes. If a drive is still busy collecting garbage, it will read slower before climbing back; the extent and duration of the drop are the result.

Idle recovery measures what downtime buys back. After a minute of sustained writes, the drive sits idle for anywhere from zero to five minutes, then a 10-second write burst, logged second by second, shows whether background cleanup freed enough blocks to run faster.

Checkpoint interference shows what an AI checkpoint does to anything else sharing the drive. A light 4K read load, which stands in for inference or database queries, runs alone, and then again during a checkpoint save. Each read is timed from when it was due, not when it was sent, so reads stuck waiting behind a stalled one count too.

Lastly, we test thermals. Drive temperature is tracked through the hour-long sustained write test and charted against the drive's own warning and critical thresholds.

The full process means every drive is treated the same and compared fairly, with a method built to give the same results run after run. This allows us to provide a level playing field built on accurate and repeatable performance characterization.

#10603. Scientists tracked kids for 8 years — the screen time result was unexpected

A Finnish study has found an unexpected association between screen use and cognitive performance. Children who accumulated more screen time as they grew up tended to show better cognitive processing during adolescence. One of the researchers cautions against viewing screen time as entirely harmful and says the goal should be to balance physical activity with screen use that encourages active thinking.

Low levels of physical activity among adolescents remain a significant public health concern. Sedentary behavior has been associated with poorer academic achievement, while physical activity is known to support brain function, particularly in adults. Much less is known, however, about how physical activity and sedentary behavior during childhood and adolescence relate to cognitive processing later in adolescence.

These years are especially important because the brain is still developing. Factors that shape cognitive abilities during childhood and adolescence may have lasting effects that extend into adulthood, potentially influencing education and working life.

Researchers examined how physical activity, sedentary behavior, and screen time from childhood through adolescence were related to cognitive processing in the teenage years. They also looked for differences between girls and boys and investigated whether the intensity of physical activity played a role.

More Screen Time Linked to Better Cognitive Processing

The results showed that greater screen time beginning in childhood was associated with better cognitive processing in adolescence.

"The findings suggest that screen time can support children's and adolescents' cognitive processing. Presumably, the essential point here is what kind of things they do in their screen time. Teachers and parents should encourage children to use devices and screens in such ways that promote active thinking, problem-solving, creativity and learning," states Doctoral Researcher Petri Jalanko from the University of Jyväskylä.

The findings suggest that the type of screen activity may be important. Digital activities that involve learning, creativity, problem-solving, or other forms of active mental engagement could potentially help explain the association observed in the study.

"We should not regard screen time solely as harmful but seek balance between physical activity and screen time that promotes active thinking," Jalanko summarizes.

Physical Activity Showed Different Links in Girls and Boys

The relationship between physical activity and cognition was more complicated.

Among girls, greater amounts of light intensity physical activity since childhood were associated with better working memory during adolescence. Among boys, greater participation in guided physical activity from childhood through adolescence was linked to better working memory.

The researchers also found an unexpected pattern involving unsupervised exercise. Lower levels of self-reported unsupervised physical activity were associated with better cognitive processing in adolescence.

By contrast, physical activity and sedentary time measured using a device that tracked heart rate and movement were not associated with cognitive processing. One possible explanation is that these sensors can measure movement and physiological activity but cannot determine exactly what a person is doing during periods of activity or inactivity.

"Our study indicates that the connections of physical activity and sedentary behavior to cognitive processing are complex and depend on the sex, the type and assessment method of physical activity and sedentary time. Moreover, boys and girls may benefit from different types of physical activity in view of brain health," Jalanko adds.

More Research Is Needed to Determine Cause and Effect

The researchers emphasize that the findings show associations rather than proving that screen time or particular types of physical activity directly cause changes in cognitive performance.

"However, we need more intervention studies to find out causal relations and sex differences in this respect. Moreover, it is important to examine more specifically the effects of the intensity of physical activity on changes taking place in cognitive processing."

The results come from an eight-year follow-up of the PANIC study on children's physical activity and nutrition. The current analysis included 124 girls and 136 boys with an average age of 15.8 years.

Researchers measured physical activity and sedentary behavior using both survey questionnaires and a device that combined heart rate and movement measurements. Learning, attention, and working memory were evaluated using the CogState test battery.

The research was published in the journal Pediatric Exercise Science.

#10600. Tiny robots powered by light can hunt down and collect bacteria

Robots small enough to operate in the microbial world could give scientists a new way to directly handle objects that are impossible to manipulate by hand. These tiny machines are around 50 times smaller than the diameter of a human hair, bringing researchers closer to the long-pursued goal of interacting directly with the microscopic world.

That capability could be especially useful for biological materials in water, including individual cells and bacteria. Precisely controlling and moving objects at this scale has been a persistent challenge. The newly developed nanorobots show that it is already possible to collect bacteria, transport them, and release them at selected locations.

One of the biggest obstacles in developing machines this small is finding an effective way to propel and control them. At Julius-Maximilians-Universität Würzburg (JMU), a research team led by Professor Bert Hecht has been developing a solution that uses the recoil produced by individual photons to move microscopic devices known as microdrones.

The devices contain as many as four plasmonic nanoantennas. These antennas absorb light with a particular color and helicity, then emit that light in a specific direction. Redirecting each photon creates a tiny recoil force, similar in principle to the recoil produced when a bullet is fired. Because the microdrones have so little mass, those extremely small forces can generate substantial acceleration and speed.

For the latest work, the researchers reduced the size of their light-powered robots even further, producing devices smaller than one micrometer. Simplifying the steering system was an important part of achieving that size while retaining propulsion based on photon recoil.

The new control method takes advantage of nanoscale antenna wires built into the robot. These wires naturally tend to align with the polarization direction of incoming light. By changing the light's polarization, the researchers can control which direction the nanorobot faces. At the same time, photon recoil continues to propel it forward, creating a steering system that works somewhat like the directional control used in larger vehicles.

"In essence, we have built a light-driven nanorobot that can track down and collect bacteria," says Jin Qin, lead experimental scientist of the study. "By simplifying the design, we reached a size at which these robots can operate directly in the microbial world - almost like microscopic cleaning devices."

The robots are also highly maneuverable. They can make extremely fast 90° turns, helping them scan broad areas of a sample in an organized and efficient way. They can also selectively capture, carry, and release substantial numbers of bacteria.

Under controlled laboratory conditions, this means the nanorobots can effectively "clean" microscopic environments. They gather bacteria from one area and deposit them at specifically chosen locations.

"This is a striking example of how light can be used not only to observe the microscopic world, but also to actively shape it," adds Bert Hecht. "The idea of tiny robotic cleaners may sound futuristic, but we are already demonstrating the physical principles that make it possible."

The robots remain fully maneuverable even while carrying larger groups of bacteria, although their speed decreases somewhat under the added load. That ability to keep functioning while transporting larger bacterial clusters points to possible future uses in microbiology, biomedical research, and precise manipulation of materials at the microscopic scale.

#10599. Data-Driven Pages That Never Go Stale: A Practical Guide

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every developer has shipped the other kind of page: a lovingly written article or landing page that peaks on launch day and then quietly rots. Screenshots age, statistics expire, rankings slide. Data-driven pages play the opposite game. Built from a living dataset, they republish themselves daily, grow to thousands of URLs, and answer questions nobody had asked when the site launched. This guide breaks down how they work, using a real fan-run stats site as the running example: one public dataset in, more than ten thousand fresh pages out.

Static pages lose ground because the web moves and they do not. Facts expire, competitors publish fresher answers, and search engines reward pages whose information matches today. A page that cannot update itself must be rewritten by hand, so most sites update a few pages and abandon the rest.

Freshness is not one rule for the whole web; it depends on the query. Nobody needs a CSS tutorial rewritten weekly, but statistics, prices, availability and anything with a season attached decays on a schedule. Developers misjudge this constantly, treating every page like documentation when half their catalogue is really a perishable good. Serve a three-month-old stat as if it were current and readers learn the most damaging lesson a site can teach: do not trust the numbers here.

Then there is the maintenance maths. A hundred hand-written pages each needing one review a year is a hundred small debts, and the interest compounds as the catalogue grows. The team updates the flagship pages, promises to return for the rest, and quietly never does. Data-driven architecture is really a staffing decision disguised as a technical one: humans design the system once, and the system does the updating forever.

A data-driven site has three layers: a dataset that refreshes on a schedule, templates that turn records into pages, and URL patterns that map every meaningful combination users might search for. Change the data and every page changes; improve a template and ten thousand pages improve at once.

The running example makes this concrete. A fan-built Overwatch stats tracker republishes Blizzard's public hero data on a daily cycle. From that one feed it generates hero profiles, head-to-head matchup pages and multi-condition tier lists: 10,291 URLs in its sitemap, across six languages, with matchup pages alone accounting for 8,586 of them. Nobody wrote those pages. Somebody designed the system that writes them, which is a very different, and much more scalable, job.

The example runs on Next.js behind a CDN, but the stack is the least important layer of the three. The same pipeline feeding WordPress, Astro or a static site generator would look identical from the reader's side of the glass. Choose boring technology you already know, and spend your originality budget on the dataset and the templates, because that is where readers will feel the difference.

Generated pages stay useful when every URL must earn its existence with data. Set minimum thresholds before a page is published, give each template unique modules its siblings do not have, and noindex combinations with nothing to say. The line between a resource and spam is not automation; it is emptiness.

Quality gates work best when they are public and mechanical. The example site publishes its tier formula openly: 70% of the score from win rate, 30% from pick rate, and a hard floor that excludes heroes picked in under 0.5% of matches from topping the ranking, because a tiny sample cannot prove anything. That single floor is editorial policy expressed as code. Your version might be a minimum number of data points, a minimum price history, or a minimum review count — the number matters less than the fact that the gate exists and applies to every page equally.

Each template also needs modules its siblings cannot copy. A hero profile carries a seven-day trend and its best and worst matchups; a matchup page carries the head-to-head history; a tier list carries the movement since yesterday. Add a short human layer on top — a methodology page explaining the scoring, changelogs narrating what moved — and the catalogue stops reading as output and starts reading as a publication. Google's scaled-content rules target pages manufactured to manipulate rankings with little value; a page that answers a real query with real, current data is the opposite of that. The honest test for any generated URL is simple: if it vanished tonight, would a searcher lose an answer?

Start embarrassingly small: one dataset, one entity type, one template. A football club's results archive, a property portal's sold prices, your own product catalogue — anything structured and refreshed beats a grand plan. Prove one template earns traffic, then add combinations, languages and dashboards in that order.

What is programmatic SEO? Programmatic SEO means generating many pages from a structured dataset using templates, instead of writing each page by hand. Done well, each page answers a distinct search with real data. Done badly, it produces thousands of near-identical pages, which is where its reputation suffers.

Is programmatic SEO against Google's rules? No. Google's spam policies target scaled content created mainly to manipulate rankings, with little or no value for users. Pages generated from real data that answer real queries are publishing. The deciding question is whether a page helps its reader, not how it was produced.

How often should the data behind pages refresh? Match the refresh to how fast the underlying facts change. Sports and game statistics justify daily pulls, prices often need hourly checks, and reference data may only move monthly. Refreshing faster than reality changes wastes crawl budget and makes 'updated' stamps meaningless.

Do generated pages need unique written text? They need unique value, which is not the same thing. Identical paragraphs on ten thousand pages add nothing, but unique data, comparisons and trends are content in their own right. Add short human-written context per template, and let the data carry the differences between pages.

How many pages should a first project launch with? Launch with the smallest set that proves the template: one entity type, a few hundred pages at most. Validate that search engines index them and users engage before adding combinations and languages. Scaling an unproven template just manufactures tomorrow's cleanup job at industrial size.

The sites quietly winning the long game are not publishing more; they are publishing systems. One honest dataset, one well-built template and strict quality gates will outlast a hundred hand-written pages competing on freshness alone. Try it this week: take one dataset you already own, design a single template with four real modules, and ship fifty pages. Watch what the data does next.

#10597. Scientists turn DNA into a memory device that uses 100x less power

DNA serves as the genetic blueprint for every living organism, but it is also an extraordinarily dense way to store information. A single gram can hold about 215 million gigabytes of data. Bringing that remarkable storage capacity into electronics could lead to more efficient data centers, faster processing and systems capable of handling increasingly complex information.

The challenge has been finding a way to make biological DNA function effectively alongside electronic materials. Penn State researchers have now developed an approach designed to overcome that incompatibility.

The work, published in Advanced Functional Materials and the subject of a patent application, relies on two key components. One is synthetic DNA, made from commercially available, chemically engineered molecules arranged into short genetic sequences tailored for specific electronic requirements. The other is crystalline perovskite, a semiconductor already used in technologies including solar cells, lasers and data storage devices.

"Biology and electronics are different domains," said Kavya S. Keremane, co-corresponding author and postdoctoral researcher in materials science and engineering at Penn State. "Bridging these two fields required developing an entirely new materials platform that allows them to function seamlessly together. By combining the information storage capabilities of DNA with the exceptional electronic properties of perovskite semiconductors, we created a bio-hybrid system that fundamentally changes how low-power memory devices can be designed."

Using these materials, the team created a memory resistor, known as a "memristor," that operates with very little energy. Unlike ordinary resistors, which maintain a set resistance to electrical current in devices ranging from cell phones to space shuttles and lose their stored information when power disappears, memristors can preserve a record of previous electrical activity. They can remember the direction in which current previously flowed even after the power source is removed.

That ability allows information to be stored and processed in the same place, resembling the way neurons function in the brain. Such an arrangement could support more simultaneous and sophisticated forms of data processing. According to the researchers, however, practical commercial systems would still require enough storage capacity and electrical power to become costly and inefficient without DNA's ability to pack enormous amounts of information into a very small space while consuming little energy.

"As the demand for artificial intelligence (AI) grows, we need a new strategy for low-power, high-storage devices," said Bed Poudel, co-corresponding author and research professor of materials science and engineering at Penn State.

Poudel said AI and other emerging technologies are expected to depend increasingly on neuromorphic computing, which is designed to operate more like the human brain. Such systems can evaluate multiple inputs at once while making decisions informed by previous experiences and future priorities.

"Usually, it takes more power to store more information. Our device, however, consumes 100 times less power and the storage capacity is higher than traditional storage devices, like flash drives."

To construct the device, the researchers added silver nanoparticles to a layer of customized DNA sequences -- specially designed to be of certain compositions and lengths -- that was integrated with thin films of perovskite.

This technique, called "doping," involves introducing a small amount of another material to produce specific properties. In this case, adding the silver nanoparticles allowed the DNA to conduct electricity while also helping its molecular units line up in a more orderly arrangement.

Synthetic DNA offered another important advantage over natural DNA. Unlike natural DNA -- long, entangled strands that behave like wet spaghetti when handled -- short and rigid pieces of synthetic DNA can be arranged with much greater precision at extremely small scales.

According to co-author Neela H. Yennawar, research professor and director of the Penn State Huck Institutes of the Life Sciences' Biomolecular Interactions Core Facility, molecularly engineered DNA can provide structural organization, adjustable electrical conductivity and functional control that natural DNA cannot achieve when incorporated into thin films.

"We can computationally determine exactly which sequences we need and how long they should be, and then we can rationally design them with synthetic DNA," Yennawar said. "These structures can be systematically doped with silver and other ions and engineered to interface seamlessly with perovskites -- transforming DNA from a biological macromolecule into a programmable, multifunctional nanomaterials platform."

When combined, the silver-doped DNA and perovskite formed bio-hybrid pathways that directed the flow of electrical current through the device.

The researchers found that electrons moved reliably when they applied less than 0.1 volt -- for comparison, standard U.S. outlets have 120 volts -- and the device responded predictably when the direction of the current was changed.

The carefully designed DNA structures, combined with the perovskite, also helped make the device unusually stable. According to the team, it continued operating consistently at temperatures approaching 250 degrees Fahrenheit and remained functional at room temperature for more than six weeks, substantially exceeding the performance standards of existing perovskite-based memory storage devices.

The researchers also reported that the new system could perform the same memory function as comparable technologies while consuming only one-tenth as much power. That level of efficiency could make the approach especially attractive for future electronics designed to handle large amounts of information with lower energy demands.

"Using just the DNA or just perovskite alone did not produce near as robust a result as the combination," Keremane said. "It's this combination that enables a very high memory storage density that requires very little power."

A New Direction for Bio-Inspired Electronics

The team now plans to improve the technology further and explore additional uses for bio-inspired electronic systems.

"Nature has the solution -- we just have to find it and apply it," Poudel said. "This work of integrating DNA into electronics to do amazing things gives a glimpse into what is possible."

In addition to Keremane, Yennawar and Poudel, other Penn State co-authors include co-corresponding author Luyao Zheng, postdoctoral research in materials science and engineering; Haodong Wu, doctoral student in materials science and engineering; Jiamao Zheng, who was a master's student in materials science and engineering at the time of research and has since graduated from Penn State; Shashank Priya, who was a professor of materials science and engineering at the time of research; and Chiranth C. Ravi, who was a master's student in the Huck Institutes of the Life Sciences at the time of research and has since graduated from Penn State. Abhinav Gorthy and co-corresponding author Rashmi Jha, chemical engineering and materials science, University of Minnesota, also contributed.

The U.S. National Science Foundation, the National Institutes of Health, Penn State and the University of Minnesota supported this research.

#10594. NASA just used satellites and debris to navigate without GPS

NASA's Starling mission has reached another milestone in autonomous spaceflight by demonstrating a system that can determine a satellite's orbital position using other objects in space as reference points rather than depending on an external navigation network.

The technology, called FALCON (Fast Autonomous Lost-in-space Catalog-based Optical Navigation), is designed to help spacecraft operate with greater independence. Such capabilities could become increasingly important as NASA pursues missions farther from Earth, including lunar satellite swarms, distributed science missions, and future human exploration.

Satellites near Earth commonly rely on GPS for navigation, but those signals may be weak, unreliable, or unavailable around the Moon and in deep space. FALCON offers an alternative by allowing spacecraft to determine where they are using what they can observe around them.

The FALCON payload is a joint flight experiment developed by NASA and EraDrive, a startup that emerged from Stanford University. The system combines EraDrive's Era-Core flight software and embedded algorithms with Starling's cameras and an onboard catalog of known satellites. Together, these tools enable GPS-independent navigation while also helping the spacecraft track activity and objects in its surroundings.

"FALCON is yet another success for the Starling demonstration mission. The results from FALCON can have far-reaching implications for on-orbit space-traffic monitoring, collision avoidance, and alternative navigation," said Roger Hunter, program manager for NASA's Small Spacecraft and Distributed Systems program at NASA's Ames Research Center in California's Silicon Valley. "The number of 'firsts' from Starling just keeps growing."

Using Satellites and Debris as Reference Points

The FALCON demonstration evaluated two related capabilities using Starling's onboard star tracker cameras. These cameras are standard spacecraft instruments that detect bright objects in space and help determine a spacecraft's orientation and position.

During position, navigation, and timing experiments, FALCON identified objects seen by Starling's cameras, including other spacecraft and orbital debris, and compared them with a publicly available catalog of known space objects maintained by the U.S. Department of War. Once the observed objects were identified and verified, FALCON used them as reference points to calculate Starling's orbit.

A separate set of experiments tested whether the spacecraft could improve orbital estimates for the objects it observed. Mission controllers loaded a catalog containing approximately 20,000 space objects and their predicted orbits onto Starling.

FALCON then compared the catalog information with observations gathered by Starling's cameras. Using those measurements, it calculated Starling's position while also refining the estimated locations of other objects in space. The resulting estimates were even more precise than the existing catalog data.

Over a period of three days, FALCON improved the known orbits of more than 200 objects without any intervention from operators on the ground.

A First for Autonomous Optical Navigation

FALCON's ability to determine its own orbit represents a first for a spacecraft navigating with optical cameras based on its position relative to other objects in space. In separate catalog updating tests, Starling also generated better onboard predictions of object positions than those supplied by ground stations.

These abilities could be especially valuable for future networks of satellites operating without GPS. Coordinated satellite systems may play an important role in supporting human activities on the surfaces of the Moon or Mars.

Precise spacecraft positioning is also essential for distributed science missions, where measurements collected from several locations in space must be accurately aligned. For space traffic management, autonomous navigation and onboard catalog updates could reduce dependence on ground-based tracking networks while improving collision avoidance.

From University Research to Commercial Technology

The FALCON experiment also demonstrates NASA's role in helping university research develop into commercial technology. The work began as a University SmallSat Technology Partnerships project before developing into EraDrive, which is now commercializing its Era-Core software and related hardware for wider use.

Starling gave the technology an opportunity to be tested under real conditions in orbit, demonstrating how advanced flight software can allow satellites to function as increasingly autonomous navigators.

Later this year, Starling, which launched in 2023, will expand the FALCON experiment using Era-Core. The mission's four spacecraft will share tracking information with one another and use those combined observations to refine their positions collectively.

NASA's Ames Research Center in California's Silicon Valley leads the Starling mission. NASA's Small Spacecraft and Distributed Systems program, based at Ames and within the Research and Technology Mission Directorate, funds and manages the Starling mission.

#10592. Man sees his cat on people’s faces everywhere after brain surgery

A man has developed a condition that makes him see the face of his cat on humans and vice versa after having surgery. Seriously.

According to MedicalXpress, a 28-year-old man by the name of Karl from the northeastern United States developed a form of palinopsia after having brain surgery back in 2025.

His form of palinopsia, prosopometamorphopsia, means that there are times when Karl has seen something earlier in the day, it can persist or reappear later on. As MedicalXpress notes, the 28-year-old has suffered this when he sees his cat, Luna, before anything else that day.

In those cases, Karl will see features of his grey tabby cat in the faces of other human beings. The main of those being his cat’s eyes, pupils, and stripes.

It isn’t always the cat that he sees, however. The report notes that the 28-year-old can see things the other way round, meaning he sees human eyes on the face of his cat. Which is pretty freaky.

“This case of Karl seeing his cat’s features in human faces and vice versa demonstrates that individual features can be pulled from memory and influence our ongoing perception,” said Brad Duchaine, a professor and chair of psychological and brain sciences and principal investigator of the Social Perception Lab at Dartmouth.

Duchaine noted that Karl’s situation is different to other cases of prosopometamorphopsia – PMO – that have been documented in the past, due to the fact he only sees features, not the full face.

The New York Post noted that PMO is sometimes often referred to as “demon face syndrome” because of how it can distort the perception of someone else’s face.

As for treatment, the 28-year-old is continuing to work with Duchaine about the condition.

#10591. IBM quantum computer solves classically intractable problem in 15 minutes

Scientists used a new error correction strategy to encode 70 logical qubits and tackle a problem considered classically intractable.

The quantum computation was completed in about 15 minutes, while leading classical computing methods would require an impractical amount of time to perform the same task.

IBM and researchers at the University of Chicago have announced a quantum computing demonstration that meets key criteria for quantum advantage. The experiment performed a calculation beyond the practical reach of leading classical simulation techniques while also providing evidence that the quantum computer produced a reliable result.

In a new paper, "Sampling hard circuits with verifiably high fidelity," the researchers describe how they achieved both goals using a newly designed form of encoded quantum circuit. The work represents one of the largest demonstrations of logical quantum computing reported so far.

The circuits and experimental results have also been made publicly available through the Quantum Advantage Tracker.

Why Quantum Results Are So Difficult to Verify

Researchers have long used a benchmark called random circuit sampling (RCS) to explore whether quantum computers can outperform conventional machines.

In simplified terms, RCS challenges a quantum computer to generate patterns that become so complicated that a classical computer cannot efficiently reproduce them. That makes the benchmark useful for testing the limits of classical simulation.

But it also creates a major problem. Once the quantum calculation becomes too difficult for a classical computer to reproduce, verifying that the quantum machine actually produced the correct result becomes increasingly difficult as well. Eventually, checking the answer can itself become infeasible unless researchers make strong assumptions about how the quantum computer behaves internally.

The IBM and University of Chicago team approached this verification problem by developing a more structured alternative to RCS.

The researchers showed that their method preserves the same computational hardness criteria associated with RCS, meaning the problem remains extremely difficult for classical computers. At the same time, the added structure allows errors to be detected during the quantum computation.

"Verification remains one of the biggest challenges in firmly establishing experimental quantum advantage," said Bill Fefferman, Associate Professor at the University of Chicago. "This experiment develops techniques to better characterize the fidelity of hard quantum states under noise, increasing confidence that the quantum computer is solving a computationally hard problem."

Soumik Ghosh, PhD student in Fefferman's group at the University of Chicago, added, "Beyond strengthening experimental validation, advances in verification have the potential to unlock practical applications for the next generation of quantum computers."

The experiment also included one of the world's largest-known demonstrations of quantum error correction.

The researchers operated 70 logical qubits. Unlike individual physical qubits, logical qubits are encoded in a way that helps protect quantum information from errors and noise.

Using those logical qubits, the team carried out 2,415 logical two-qubit operations and 468 logical "T gates," two measures that reflect the complexity of the quantum circuit.

The encoded design significantly improved reliability. Effective logical error rates were 10 times lower than the underlying physical error rates, allowing the circuit to maintain unusually high fidelity even while performing a large number of quantum operations.

IBM Says Quantum Advantage Has Entered a New Stage

"We are now firmly in the quantum advantage era," said Jay Gambetta, Director of IBM Research and IBM Fellow. "We have demonstrated a quantum computation beyond the practical reach of classical computers that establishes, with statistical confidence, a lower bound on how faithfully it was executed. This milestone gives scientists, developers, and businesses a new foundation for trusting quantum computers as they scale to problems far beyond what we can achieve classically."

The researchers found that many leading classical simulation methods would face prohibitive runtimes when attempting the same task.

The IBM quantum computer, by comparison, completed the computation in approximately 15 minutes.

A Step Toward Larger, More Trustworthy Quantum Computers

Speed alone is not enough for quantum computing to become useful at larger scales. Researchers also need ways to suppress errors and establish confidence that a quantum system produced a valid result.

This experiment advances both goals at once by combining large-scale logical quantum computing with a method for evaluating the reliability of a calculation that is already beyond practical classical simulation.

Error correction and trustworthy verification are considered essential for scaling quantum computers toward more difficult problems, making the new demonstration an important step toward that goal.

#10589. New app punishes you and your friends for spending too much time on your phone

A new app called Island is taking a different approach to reducing screen time, turning your phone usage into a farming simulator where spending too much time on your device can have consequences for you and your friends.

Unlike traditional screen time apps that block access to certain applications after a set period, Island encourages users to put their phones down by rewarding them for meeting daily limits.

The app, which launched in October 2026, lets users build and manage a virtual farm, with their progress directly tied to how well they control their screen time. However, those who struggle to stick to their limits could end up affecting their friends’ progress as well.

Developed by entrepreneurs Ashish Selvaraj and Ethan Pronev, Island uses a farming simulator to encourage users to spend less time on their devices without physically restricting access to their apps.

Users establish daily screen time limits and earn rewards for sticking to them. Meeting these goals improves an in-game “credit score,” which increases farm production and unlocks new crops, animals, and other content.

Rather than locking users out of their phones when they reach their limits, Island sends reminders, including a warning when they have 15 minutes remaining.

The app also introduces a social element that allows friends to visit each other’s islands, compete in group challenges, and hold one another accountable for their screen time habits.

One feature even allows two friends to link their islands together, meaning that if either person exceeds their daily screen time limit, both players’ farms will suffer the consequences.

Speaking to TechCrunch, Selvaraj explained that the team wanted to expand on the concept of existing productivity apps that reward users for staying focused.

New Steam game challenges players to be a banana for 100 hours

Viral alarm app forces users to complete mini-games before it will shut off

This Steam game wants you to beat it within two hours so you get a refund

Similar applications, including Hank Green’s Focus Friend, have previously used virtual companions to encourage people to spend less time on distracting apps.

However, Island takes the concept further by introducing a farming simulator with shared consequences, allowing users to experience setbacks without “having to torture your pet,” according to Selvaraj.

The developers say their approach has already shown promising results following early testing and the app’s October launch.

According to data shared by the startup, 82% of users stop using their phones when they reach their established screen time limits, while others put their devices down after receiving the 15-minute warning.

Island also reportedly has a 49% retention rate after 30 days, meaning nearly half of users are still active on the app on their 30th day after installation.

The app is currently free to use, although an optional $39.99 annual subscription allows users to establish individual screen time limits for different applications rather than relying on a single daily limit.
