#10588. Running WireGuard for a Development Team: The Hidden Cost of VPN Maintenance

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Setting up WireGuard on a small virtual server is a satisfying infrastructure task. Install the package, generate a key pair, open a UDP port, configure a peer, and connect a laptop. Within an afternoon, your staging environment can sit behind a private tunnel, and your internal dashboard can be restricted to traffic from a known address.

The initial setup is rarely the difficult part.

The real engineering work starts when the team changes. A developer joins, another leaves, someone replaces a laptop, a server needs patching, or a connection suddenly stops working. Each event creates a small maintenance task. Over time, those tasks become an operational responsibility that somebody must own.

This article examines what it takes to operate a self-hosted WireGuard VPN for a development team. We'll look at peer configuration, access management, troubleshooting, monitoring tools, and the engineering time involved. We'll also compare self-hosting with managed business VPN services so teams can make a decision based on their infrastructure needs.

WireGuard is a modern VPN protocol designed to establish encrypted connections between network peers. Its relatively small codebase, modern cryptography, and straightforward configuration make it attractive for developers who want control over their network infrastructure.

A basic WireGuard deployment typically involves:

A server with a reachable public IP address.

A private and public key pair for the server.

A UDP port through which peers communicate.

Peer configuration that defines which addresses are allowed through the tunnel.

A simplified server configuration might look like this:

The corresponding client configuration contains the client's private key and the server's public key:

These examples illustrate the basic relationship between a server and a client. A production configuration will also need appropriate firewall rules, routing, DNS settings, and a plan for distributing configuration securely.

The important distinction is that WireGuard establishes encrypted peer connections. It does not automatically provide a complete employee identity system, onboarding workflow, approval process, or audit dashboard.

Those responsibilities belong to the team operating the infrastructure.

Developers interested in using WireGuard on personal development devices can also explore SitePoint's guide to building a hardened mobile development environment with Termux, Docker, and WireGuard.

A peer is not just an entry in a configuration file. It represents a device that has permission to communicate through your network.

That makes peer management an access-control responsibility.

For a small development team, it is tempting to keep a single configuration file and update it manually whenever someone joins or leaves. This can work initially, but it becomes harder to manage as the number of devices increases.

A useful first step is to maintain a peer inventory:

The inventory should not contain private keys. Private keys must remain on their respective devices and should never be placed in a shared spreadsheet or source repository.

The inventory instead helps administrators answer practical questions:

Which device is associated with this tunnel address?

Has the peer been removed after the device was lost or replaced?

This information becomes especially important when a team uses the VPN to reach staging servers, internal dashboards, databases, or other development resources.

Manual onboarding is manageable when a team has only two or three developers. It becomes unreliable when several people join, leave, or change devices over the year.

A repeatable onboarding process should cover more than generating a key pair.

Confirm that the team member is authorized to access the environment.

Generate a key pair on the user's device.

Add the public key to the server's peer configuration.

Apply the appropriate routing and firewall permissions.

Deliver the client configuration through a secure channel.

Test access to the specific development resources the person needs.

Record the peer owner, device, and approval details.

Offboarding needs an equally clear process:

Identify every peer associated with the departing team member.

Remove the relevant public keys from the server.

Confirm that the configuration change has been applied.

Review any other credentials or allowlists that granted access.

An administrator can inspect the currently configured peers with:

The output helps identify peer public keys, transfer counters, and latest handshake information. It is useful for troubleshooting, but it should not be treated as a complete access audit or proof that a particular person is still authorized.

The broader lesson is to make access changes part of a documented workflow. A peer should not remain active simply because nobody remembers who created it.

One common network-design mistake is assuming that connecting to a VPN should give a developer access to every internal service.

A developer working on a frontend application may need access to a staging web server but not to the production database. A contractor may need access to one test environment without being able to reach internal administration tools.

WireGuard's peer configuration and the surrounding network controls should be designed around those requirements.

Separate tunnel address ranges for different groups.

Firewall rules that restrict traffic to approved services.

Cloud security groups that limit access to specific ports.

Application-level authentication for internal dashboards.

Separate production and staging network boundaries.

The exact implementation depends on the infrastructure. The key point is that VPN membership and application authorization are different controls.

A successful VPN handshake should not be treated as permission to access every resource behind the tunnel.

A VPN can appear simple when it works. Diagnosing it becomes more complicated when a developer is connecting from a home router, a hotel network, or a mobile hotspot.

Incorrect routing or AllowedIPs settings.

A server process or host becoming unavailable.

WireGuard provides useful inspection commands:

These commands help inspect peer information, check the service state, and review recent service logs. The exact systemd service name can differ depending on how the interface was configured.

Administrators should also monitor the underlying server. At minimum, that means checking:

Whether the WireGuard interface is active.

Whether disk or memory usage is approaching a limit.

Whether operating-system and package updates are pending.

A small virtual server may be inexpensive, but it is still infrastructure that needs an owner.

Teams working with alternative tunnel-based development workflows may find SitePoint's tutorial on setting up Cloudflare Tunnels with Docker Compose for persistent webhook debugging useful. It covers a different tunneling use case, but the operational questions around connectivity, service health, and troubleshooting are closely related.

A self-hosted VPN does not need to become a collection of manual tasks. A few established tools can make configuration, monitoring, and debugging more repeatable.

The core tool is, of course, WireGuard. Its command-line utilities allow administrators to inspect peers, manage interfaces, and review connection information. The official documentation is the best starting point for understanding configuration options and platform support.

Ansible can automate repeatable server administration tasks. A team could use playbooks to deploy configuration files, apply system updates, or standardize firewall settings across several VPN hosts.

Sensitive values should be handled with an appropriate secrets-management approach. Private keys should not be committed to a Git repository or stored in plaintext playbooks.

Prometheus is useful for collecting and querying metrics, while Node Exporter exposes Linux host metrics such as CPU, memory, disk, and network statistics.

These tools do not automatically provide a complete WireGuard security audit. They can, however, help identify resource problems and server-health issues before those problems become user-reported outages.

Uptime Kuma provides a self-hosted monitoring dashboard with notifications. It can help teams monitor the availability of a VPN host or related services.

A basic availability check does not prove that every VPN peer can connect successfully. For more meaningful monitoring, combine host checks with an end-to-end test from a separate network.

Wireshark and tcpdump help developers inspect network traffic when diagnosing connectivity problems.

For example, tcpdump can capture UDP traffic on the WireGuard port:

This can help determine whether packets are reaching the server. It does not decrypt WireGuard traffic or replace checks of routing, firewall rules, and peer configuration.

The right toolset depends on the size of the deployment. A team with one VPN server may only need WireGuard's own commands and basic host monitoring. A larger environment may benefit from automation, centralized metrics, and a documented incident process.

A self-hosted VPN can cost very little in direct infrastructure charges. However, the server invoice does not include the engineering time required to operate it.

Consider a hypothetical team with these assumptions:

Two people join and two leave during the year.

The team reviews or rotates device keys quarterly.

Patching and monitoring require about two hours per month.

Six troubleshooting incidents occur annually.

The estimated loaded engineering cost is $60 per hour.

The virtual server costs approximately $6 per month.

The following estimate illustrates how quickly maintenance time can exceed the infrastructure bill.

At $60 per hour, 46 hours represent approximately $2,760 in engineering time. The server adds around $72 per year, bringing the estimated total to $2,832.

These figures are illustrative, not a universal cost benchmark. A team with infrastructure automation and established monitoring may spend considerably less time. A team with frequent staff changes or strict audit requirements may spend more.

The useful exercise is to measure the work your own team performs. Track onboarding, access reviews, patching, incidents, and recovery time for a few months. That gives you a more realistic comparison than looking at hosting prices alone.

Self-hosting is not automatically the best option just because a team has developers who can configure servers.

A managed business VPN may be worth considering when the team needs centralized member management, simpler device administration, or a stable network exit address without maintaining the underlying VPN infrastructure.

Depending on the provider and plan, managed services may offer:

A central dashboard for adding and removing users.

Client applications for common operating systems.

Multi-factor authentication or identity integrations.

For example, PureVPN for Teams is one service in this category. Teams evaluating it—or any other provider—should check the current feature set, pricing, logging practices, access controls, and service terms before making a decision.

A managed service does not remove every security responsibility. The team still needs to decide who receives access, review permissions, protect user accounts, and understand how traffic and metadata are handled.

It also introduces vendor dependency and a recurring subscription cost. Those trade-offs should be considered alongside the engineering time saved.

There are situations where running WireGuard yourself is the more practical option.

You already operate infrastructure. If your team routinely patches servers, manages secrets, monitors services, and responds to incidents, maintaining a VPN may add relatively little work.

You need custom routing. A deployment that connects several private cloud networks, on-premises environments, or specialized subnets may require network controls that are easier to implement directly.

You have strict data-control requirements. Some organizations have policies that limit which third parties can handle network traffic or related metadata. Those requirements may favor infrastructure under the organization's own control.

You have a dedicated platform or security team. If someone is explicitly responsible for access management, monitoring, and incident response, self-hosting can fit naturally into existing operations.

You can automate the lifecycle. Infrastructure-as-code, configuration management, documented peer inventories, and tested recovery procedures can reduce repetitive work and make a self-hosted deployment easier to maintain.

The important distinction is between a deliberately operated service and a one-off server that nobody owns.

Before choosing an approach, ask the following questions:

Who is responsible for the VPN six or twelve months after deployment?

How many people and devices are likely to join or leave?

How are public keys and peer configurations tracked?

How quickly can access be revoked when a device is lost?

Which internal services should each developer be able to reach?

Do you need a fixed egress IP for external allowlists?

What monitoring and recovery process exists if the server fails?

Do your customers or internal policies require access logs or formal reviews?

Does your team have the time and expertise to maintain the infrastructure?

Would a managed service cost less than the engineering time and operational risk it replaces?

If your team already has mature infrastructure practices and needs custom network control, self-hosting may be a sensible choice.

If the team is small, access changes frequently, and nobody has time to own another server, a managed service may be more practical.

WireGuard is an excellent protocol, and deploying it can be a straightforward engineering task. But operating a VPN for a development team involves more than establishing an encrypted connection.

Peer management, onboarding, offboarding, network permissions, monitoring, patching, and troubleshooting all require ongoing attention.

The right choice depends on the team's infrastructure experience, security requirements, size, and available engineering time. Self-hosting offers control and flexibility; a managed service can reduce operational work. Neither option removes the need for sound access-control practices.

Before deciding, calculate the maintenance effort, identify who will own the service, and make sure the design can be maintained as the team changes.

The cost example uses illustrative assumptions rather than current vendor pricing. Managed VPN features and prices can change, so verify provider details before purchasing.

#10585. A “quantum bath” puts quantum entanglement on autopilot

Future quantum computers may depend on the ability to connect widely separated modules through distributed entanglement. Until now, creating this kind of connection has generally required active control and repeated measurements. Physicists at the Institute of Science and Technology Austria (ISTA) have now demonstrated a fully autonomous alternative based on a "quantum bath" made from correlated particles of light.

Published in Physical Review X, the experiment provides the first demonstration of a prediction proposed more than 20 years ago. The approach could eventually offer a new foundation for practical quantum technologies.

Entanglement is one of the defining phenomena of quantum physics. It allows particles or systems to share correlations that cannot be explained by classical physics. Creating distributed entanglement between physically separated qubits (quantum bits) could be important for building larger quantum computers and future quantum networks.

Previous attempts to entangle distant qubits have generally followed one of two strategies. One method sends a single, actively controlled photon from one qubit to another. Another has each qubit emit a photon, with the two photons then matched in an effort to generate entanglement.

The second approach was recognized by the 2022 Nobel Prize in Physics. However, it still depends on repeated measurements and post-selection, and even then, the process does not always successfully produce entanglement.

PhD student Alejandro Andrés-Juanes and professor Johannes Fink at the Institute of Science and Technology Austria (ISTA) worked with international collaborators to develop a different solution. Their system uses a quantum bath that automatically brings distant qubits into synchronization. In a prototype device, the researchers used a shared source of correlated light particles to entangle two separated qubits, experimentally realizing an idea that had remained theoretical for more than two decades.

Quantum entanglement can take several forms. Continuous-variable entangled states can be produced efficiently and are therefore relatively accessible. They can be compared with a pendulum, whose position and momentum change continuously.

Many useful quantum technologies, however, depend on "discrete-variable" systems. These involve 'all-or-nothing' forms of entanglement that stationary qubits can use. The challenge for the ISTA team was finding a way to connect these readily available continuous forms of entanglement with the discrete forms needed for practical applications.

"In this work, we aimed to overcome this mismatch between the readily available and the practically useful forms of entanglement," says Andrés-Juanes. "By stabilizing the entangled states remotely, our approach is fully autonomous and requires no active control or measurement."

A Quantum Bath Powered by Correlated Light

Maintaining both entanglement and quantum coherence is one of the major difficulties facing quantum computing.

The researchers addressed that problem by making the qubits' surrounding environment itself responsible for producing and stabilizing entanglement.

"In our method, the quantum bath -- meaning the qubits' environment -- is the source of entanglement. It creates a new ground state through a continuous stream of correlated photons," says Fink. "This way, the entangled qubit state is stabilized, even beyond the qubits' own 'lifetime', and remains always available as a resource for further quantum processing. This makes the approach conceptually significant."

Because the entangled state remains available, researchers can access it whenever it is needed. That differs from temporary entanglement, which has to be used during the brief period when it exists.

To couple the qubits with the entangled photon source, the researchers relied on microwave photons. These low-energy light particles are especially useful for manipulating quantum information and are already central to leading superconducting-qubit technology.

Optical photons serve a different role and are commonly used in optics and atomic physics. They may also become important for carrying quantum information between distant quantum computers through fiber optics, an area that the Fink group at ISTA is also investigating.

The researchers needed to confirm that the two qubits were actually synchronized inside the quantum bath. To do that, they used quantum tomography, a technique that reconstructs a quantum system by examining many different 'slices' of its behavior.

"Qubits can be in a superposition of states, but all these states collapse when we measure them, leaving us with a 0 or 1 state," says Andrés-Juanes.

Quantum tomography allowed the researchers to perform measurements lasting only 20-80 nanoseconds and use those observations to investigate the qubits' underlying states. A nanosecond is one billionth of a second.

A 20-Year Prediction Becomes an Experiment

By successfully entangling two isolated qubits through a quantum bath, the ISTA researchers created a proof-of-concept laboratory prototype for the long-standing theoretical proposal.

"We present a relatively simple method that could be scaled up to synchronize multiple distant qubits," says Andrés-Juanes.

The new approach is promising, but it is not yet as efficient as methods that actively control qubit states. "Our method currently transfers about 10% of the bath's available entanglement."

The researchers suggest that one reason the idea took more than two decades to demonstrate is that the original theory was developed under idealized conditions that are difficult to reproduce experimentally.

"Our experiments helped us reveal several factors that may have prevented scientists from designing a functional quantum bath using a single source of correlated photons for distributed entanglement," says Fink.

The prototype developed at ISTA could provide new opportunities for quantum-optics experiments. It may also contribute to efforts to expand quantum processors and ultimately move them closer to fault-tolerant operation.

Materials provided by Institute of Science and Technology Austria. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10581. Phison Pascari X201P 15.36TB SSD Review: Fast PCIe 5.0 write performance for AI and ingest servers

Phison’s Pascari X201P meets its rated specs and pairs 12 GB/s sequential writes with a steady 600K random write IOPS, making short work of AI checkpoints. It gives ground at low and moderate queue depths, where QD1 writes and mixed workloads lag, and it ran warm on our bench.

Mixed-workload performance trails at middle QDs

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

Phison is serious about enterprise storage, and the Pascari X201P was not released lightly; it came in the night, under NDA, alongside this week’s Supercomputing 2026 event. The drive is part of the Performance X-Series, which handles intensive workloads where consistency is the name of the game. We were nevertheless pleasantly surprised by its write performance, with a steady 12 GB/s for sequential and over 600,000 random write IOPS, considering it’s marketed at the 1 drive write per day (DWPD) endurance point.

Part of that surprise comes from the fact that this is Phison’s read-intensive PCIe 5.0 data center drive, a successor to the X200P. It comes in the U.2 form factor — which can be more convenient in many cases — from 1.92TB to 30.72TB, and an E3.S version from 3.84TB, in both single- and dual-port models. It stands next to its mixed-use sibling, the X201E, which is the one rated for 3 DWPD. What makes the X201P exciting, in part, is its own 16-channel X2 controller and the flash that’s inside.

This drive is aimed at AI, analytics, and database servers, with its case made in the 4K 70/30 mix at higher queue depths. It passed Phison’s one-million-IOPS mark at QD128 in our testing of the 15.36TB model, and the drive met all of its rated specs, though its random read rating required polled I/O to reach on our bench. It faced challenges with QD1 writes, log commits, and the middle range of our mixed sweeps. Given its blend of performance, capacity, and endurance, we consider it a very competent entry from the company that was perhaps better known in the consumer SSD market until recently.

The drive met or beat every rated performance figure that we can test, which should be the target of any enterprise SSD released today. If there’s an exception, it’s due to how we test; Phison lists a ceiling of 3,250K IOPS for 4K random read, which is higher than our bench allows with our interrupt-driven I/O, which we use rather than a polled approach. We also run a polled test on every drive for cases like this, and our result was 3,283K IOPS at the same queue depth Phison specifies, which shows the drive does exceed its specification.

Datacenter NVMe SSD v2.0, with v2.6 telemetry features

PCIe 5.0 x4, single port (2x2 dual-port models offered)

ISE, SED (TCG Opal 2.02) or non-SED; AES-XTS 256-bit

512-byte and 4,096-byte, each with optional metadata

Six: 25 W, 22 W, 20 W, 18 W, 15 W and 12 W maximum

Host-controlled thermal management supported

Our sample is in the U.2 form factor, 15mm thick. We have the single-port SKU, ISE model XP208H0815T3P3216T310, with firmware X2WM6026. Some data we previously saw from the drive was reportedly on X2WM6025, but we confirmed X2WM6026 for all relevant results. We mounted the drive on a PCIe 5.0 x4 add-in card adapter for U.2, standing upright in the slot.

Phison did not provide firm hardware details, but we were able to discover some things on our own. The flash was probably the easiest, as the company claimed it was a 321-layer part. That can only be SK hynix’s V9 TLC, which is not flash we’ve tested before in any product. It’s exceptionally fast and uses a three-deck design, which is relatively new for TLC flash. The flash could be credited with some of the exceptional performance measurements we see on the drive.

As for the proprietary controller, we’ve heard that it’s using a combination of the standard Arm Cortex-R5 — these would be the main cores — with AndesCore RISC-V on the side. We’ve seen the latter before on the Phison E28 consumer SSD controller, which did have some enterprise-like aspects to it. The E28 is the newer design, built on a 6nm process with Andes’ newer N45 cores alongside the N25F, while the X2 is a 12nm part that, as far as we can tell, sticks with N25F-class co-processors.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

In this case, the X2 has twice the flash channels, which enables a superior level of performance out of TLC flash, as in enterprise you do not rely on pSLC. That mix of cores sets the X2 apart from other 16-channel PCIe 5.0 enterprise controllers: Marvell’s Bravera SC5 and Silicon Motion’s SM8366 rely on Arm cores throughout, whereas Phison pairs its Cortex-R5 cores with small RISC-V co-processors.

#10579. Scientists just made quantum computer operations 1,000 times faster

Quantum computers remain highly vulnerable to errors and tiny disturbances from their surroundings. The longer a quantum operation takes to complete, the more time there is for those errors to build up. Researchers at Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden have now developed a method that can perform a broad range of advanced quantum operations more than a thousand times faster. The advance tackles a major obstacle in the field and could help move quantum computing closer to becoming fault-tolerant.

Quantum computers could eventually transform areas such as drug discovery, energy technology, cryptography, artificial intelligence, and logistics. Before that can happen, however, these machines need to become much more dependable.

A major challenge is that quantum computations can be disrupted by extremely small environmental effects. Electrical noise, cosmic radiation, and overheating can all introduce errors while information is being processed.

Traditional computers can experience errors too, but decades of development have produced reliable error correction methods that can quickly detect and repair them. Quantum systems are much harder to protect because the information they use is extraordinarily delicate.

"The fundamental building blocks of quantum computers, known as qubits, are so sensitive that even the smallest disturbance can cause the quantum state to deviate from the target, resulting in the loss of information. If too many errors accumulate before they can be corrected, the computation can fail," says Lei Du, researcher in Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden, and lead author of the theoretical study published in the journal Physical Review Letters.

A Different Way to Protect Quantum Information

To make quantum computing more resilient and eventually fault-tolerant, researchers are investigating new ways to shield quantum information from errors. One promising strategy uses so-called bosonic quantum codes*.

Instead of assigning quantum information to individual qubits, this approach stores it in microwave fields inside superconducting circuits.

"Rather than storing quantum information in individual qubits, bosonic codes encode information in the microwave fields found within superconducting circuits. This approach has been shown to provide stronger protection against certain types of errors," explains Tangyou Huang, researcher in Quantum Technology at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Quantum Operations More Than 1,000 Times Faster

Working with bosonic quantum codes is not simple. Creating and controlling the required quantum states has traditionally involved guiding a quantum system through thousands of repeated driving cycles.

That process can take considerable time, and every additional cycle creates another opportunity for outside disturbances to interfere with the calculation. In quantum computing, speed is therefore closely tied to reliability.

Chalmers researchers Lei Du and Tangyou Huang have now proposed a different strategy. Rather than constructing the desired quantum states one small piece at a time, their method can perform a wide variety of operations much faster.

"Our method shows that a diverse range of quantum operations on bosonic states can be completed within a single driving cycle, rather than the several thousand cycles that have been required previously. This makes the operations both faster and more efficient, while reducing the risk that disturbances will corrupt the information before the process is finished. It represents an important step towards fault-tolerant quantum computers," says Lei Du.

The new approach is built around Quantum lattice gates, a recently proposed universal set of quantum gates developed by the same research team.

These gates act somewhat like shortcuts. Instead of requiring a long sequence of repeated control steps, they can allow the intended quantum operation to be completed in just one driving cycle. That could make the process faster, simpler, and less vulnerable to errors.

"You can think of it like building a large Lego castle. Instead of assembling it brick by brick and risking mistakes along the way, quantum lattice gates act like pre-built Lego modules that can be connected quickly and efficiently," says Tangyou Huang.

Designed for Superconducting Quantum Computers

The technique is especially well suited to superconducting quantum computers, which are among the most prominent technologies being developed in the international push toward large-scale quantum computing.

Chalmers University of Technology is also using superconducting technology as it develops a 100-qubit quantum computer.

"A key advantage of our approach is that it can be implemented using existing superconducting quantum circuit platforms. We are already discussing possible experimental realizations with colleagues at Chalmers, and we hope to see a demonstration of the method in the near future," says Tangyou Huang.

The researchers say the work addresses a central problem facing the field: efficiently producing and controlling quantum states that are capable of helping correct errors.

"Our results address one of the major bottlenecks in the field: how to quickly and reliably create and control the error-correcting quantum states that could play an important role in future quantum computers," says Lei Du.

More About Bosonic Codes, Quantum Lattice Gates and Floquet Control

*Bosonic quantum codes store quantum information in the states of, for example, microwave or optical resonators rather than in individual qubits. They are considered a promising tool for quantum error correction because they can provide built-in protection against certain types of errors.

Quantum operations are needed to process that stored information. Quantum lattice gates are a recently proposed collection of basic building blocks that can be used to control bosonic quantum states. By combining and designing these gates, researchers can carry out a wide range of more complicated quantum operations.

One method for implementing these operations is known as Floquet control, which uses periodic control signals to drive a quantum system. Earlier Floquet-based techniques have generally depended on slower processes involving many repeated driving cycles. The approach developed at Chalmers can instead perform quantum lattice gates directly in a single driving cycle, making some operations more than a thousand times faster.

The scientific paper "Single-Period Floquet Control of Bosonic Codes with Quantum Lattice Gates" has been published in Physical Review Letters. The authors are Tangyou Huang, Lei Du, and Lingzhen Guo. The researchers are affiliated with Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden and Tianjin University in China.

The research was funded by the National Natural Science Foundation of China (NSFC), the Wallenberg Centre for Quantum Technology (WACQT), and the Knut and Alice Wallenberg Foundation.

Materials provided by Chalmers University of Technology. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10578. The Design Workflow Is Broken. Here Is How Vitalii Khomenko Thinks It Should Be Fixed

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

With 250+ client engagements across both agency and embedded in-house roles, Vitalii Khomenko has seen product teams from the inside in a way few designers have. Here he makes the case for a new kind of design workflow built for the AI era, and explains why the window to adapt is narrower than most founders think.

For the last decade, every startup conversation about speed eventually became a conversation about engineering. Hiring more developers, cutting scope, choosing a simpler stack, it was almost always a dev-side problem. Vitalii Khomenko, Principal Designer at Orizon Design and founder of Fluente LLC, argues that's no longer true. And he has a specific view on what teams should do instead.

What makes Khomenko's perspective worth taking seriously isn't just seniority. It's the specific shape of his experience. Over a career spanning 250+ client engagements, he has worked both as an external specialist brought in to deliver and as an embedded designer who became the single connective thread between product, brand, and execution. That dual vantage point is rare. Most designers live on one side of that line. Khomenko has operated extensively on both, which gives him an unusually clear read on where friction actually originates inside product teams, and where it doesn't.

His work spans early-stage startups finding their first visual language and established brands managing complex, multi-disciplinary design systems. Across that range, certain patterns repeat. And the pattern that started becoming impossible to ignore this year pointed him toward a conclusion that is reshaping how he thinks a modern design workflow should actually be structured.

The bottleneck didn't disappear. It moved. And the workflow needs to move with it.

When shipping code was slow, the existing design workflow made sense. Multiple rounds of revision, user testing, iteration, all of that fit naturally into the window while engineering caught up. That window is now gone, and most design workflows were never updated to reflect it.

"Founders are asking design teams to move at the same speed as an AI-assisted dev pipeline, and design doesn't compress the same way code does," Khomenko says. The result is a workflow that was built around a constraint that no longer exists, applied to a pipeline that has fundamentally changed around it.

The forced choice between polish and speed that product teams are now navigating isn't new as a concept. What's new is how stark it has become, and how directly it traces back to a workflow problem rather than a people problem. "Because development is cheap and fast now, there's constant pressure to ship the next thing immediately," he explains. "But the products that actually retain users are still the ones where the details were considered carefully. Fewer revision rounds isn't a process improvement if it means shipping avoidable friction."

Khomenko has watched this play out across enough teams, at enough stages, to be specific about where the existing workflow breaks down. The issue isn't design judgment. It's where that judgment sits in the process, and what format it's expected to produce at the end.

To understand Khomenko's proposed fix, it helps to understand exactly where the current workflow stalls. For most product teams, the end of the design process looks like this: a designer produces polished, dev-ready screens inside Figma, and hands that file to engineering. Engineering then interprets those screens, translates the visual decisions into code, and builds from there.

That translation step, Figma file to working code, is where the pipeline now breaks down. AI-assisted engineering has made the building part dramatically faster. But the translation step hasn't changed. An engineer still has to read a Figma file, interpret spacing, extract assets, make judgment calls about component structure, and write the implementation from scratch. That process takes the same amount of time it always did.

"The manual workflow inside Figma simply isn't keeping up with AI-boosted engineering," Khomenko says. "The design handoff is quietly becoming the thing everyone is waiting on."

The result is a pipeline where everything around the handoff has accelerated, and the handoff itself has become the single point of drag. Figma, in other words, was built for a world where engineering was the slow part. It wasn't designed to feed a pipeline where engineering moves at AI speed.

This is the core of what Khomenko is proposing, and it's a more significant structural shift than it might first appear.

In his workflow, Figma doesn't disappear. It moves. Rather than serving as both the exploration environment and the final delivery artifact, Figma becomes the visual exploration layer exclusively: the place where brand identity is established, layout logic is worked through, and design direction is validated. It remains the right tool for figuring out what something should look and feel like.

But the final delivery, the artifact that actually goes to engineering, is code. Specifically, components built into prepared libraries that engineering can work from and scale directly, without any translation step in between.

The difference in implementation speed is the point. When a designer delivers a coded component library rather than a Figma file, engineering doesn't need to interpret anything. The decisions are already expressed in the language they're building in. What previously required an engineer to read, interpret, and re-create from a visual reference now arrives ready to use. In a pipeline running on AI-assisted development, that difference compounds fast.

"Figma is still the right place to explore what something should look and feel like," Khomenko says. "But handing over a Figma file and asking engineering to implement it is now the slow path. Delivering in code is the fast path. That's the shift."

Executing this workflow requires a different kind of designer, and Khomenko is direct about that. The UI/UX designer becoming a Design Engineer is, in his assessment, not a distant possibility. It is a matter of time, and the teams treating it as a future consideration rather than a present reality are the ones most likely to find themselves stuck at the handoff.

But Khomenko is equally clear about what this shift doesn't mean. Delivering in code is one part of the role, not the whole of it. The full design cycle isn't going anywhere. Applying branding to imagery, producing animations, building marketing materials, these remain essential creative tasks that require a broad and varied toolkit well beyond code. "The designer of the near future isn't just generating code," he says. "They're moving across the full cycle, brand, motion, marketing, product, with design engineering as an important and growing part of that stack, not a replacement for it."

What changes is that code-ready delivery becomes a core expectation within that stack. The designer who can move fluidly from visual exploration in Figma to component delivery in code, while also carrying the brand thinking, motion, and marketing creativity that differentiates products at the surface level users actually experience, is the profile that the new workflow demands.

Khomenko is candid that no single version of this workflow has fully standardized yet. Every team is figuring it out differently. The tools are evolving, the handoff expectations are shifting, and the processes teams are building around all of it vary considerably. There is no off-the-shelf playbook.

But this is precisely where his 250+ engagements become more than a credential. They're a dataset. Having watched enough teams, at enough stages, through enough cycles of tooling change, Khomenko can see through the noise of individual variation and identify what's directionally true even when the specifics are still in flux. "Each team's workflow is really custom at this stage," he says. "But some general patterns of how this is going to look in the near future are already visible."

Those patterns consistently point in the same direction: design judgment moving earlier, final delivery moving into code, and the designer's role expanding in technical range while staying broad in creative scope. The exact tools and sequences will keep shifting. The underlying logic of the workflow Khomenko is describing is already taking shape in the teams paying closest attention.

For founders and product leads, the workflow Khomenko is describing has direct implications for how teams are staffed, budgeted, and structured.

Front-load design judgment. The biggest leverage in the new workflow isn't faster execution, it's better problem definition before generation starts. Bring senior design thinking in earlier, when it can shape what gets built rather than polish what's already done.

Separate exploration from delivery. Figma and the visual exploration phase serve a distinct purpose from the final delivery layer. Treat them as separate stages with separate expectations, rather than collapsing them into a single step expected to produce everything.

Shift the delivery expectation to code. Whether that means hiring for Design Engineer range, developing it internally, or restructuring handoffs, the end of the design pipeline needs to arrive in a form engineering can work from directly. The translation step is where time goes to die, and it's now the most avoidable part of the process.

Protect the full creative stack. Design engineering matters more than it ever has. It is not, however, the whole job. Teams that reduce design to code generation will lose the brand thinking, motion, and marketing creativity that differentiates products at the surface level users actually experience.

Stay flexible. The workflow is still forming. Teams that build rigidity into their process now will find themselves redoing that work within a year. The teams getting this right are the ones building enough adaptability into their structures to absorb what's still coming.

"The Figma handoff made sense when engineering was the slow part," Khomenko says. "Engineering isn't the slow part anymore. Delivering in code isn't the future of design workflow. For the teams paying attention, it's already the present."

I write about AI and software development from the angle that usually gets skipped: what a spec sheet or benchmark actually implies once you try to build with it. Find me on LinkedIn.

#10575. KFC just released fried cheese with four different colors inside

KFC has unveiled a bizarre new fried cheese snack that looks ordinary on the outside, but reveals four different colors of melted cheese when pulled apart.

The fast food giant is no stranger to unusual menu creations, but its latest offering in Malaysia might be one of its most colorful yet.

Called Colourful Cheezy Cheese, the new item launched on November 18, 2026, featuring a crispy, golden-brown exterior packed with stretchy, multicolored cheese.

According to local promotional listings, Colourful Cheezy Cheese is available at participating KFC restaurants across Malaysia for RM7.49, equivalent to approximately $1.80 USD.

The snack resembles a large, breaded mozzarella stick flattened into a patty. However, pulling it apart reveals four different colors of gooey cheese rather than the usual white or yellow filling.

Despite the unusual appearance, KFC hasn’t publicly detailed how the different colors are achieved or whether they have distinct flavors.

The product is also being offered as part of KFC Malaysia’s new Holiday Bucket meals, with six-piece and ten-piece chicken bundles including one or two colorful cheese patties, respectively.

It’s another unusual creation from KFC’s international restaurants, which have been experimenting with increasingly outrageous menu items throughout 2026.

In August, KFC China left the chain’s main social media account baffled after unveiling a fried chicken burger featuring Oreo-flavored buns and sweet dipping sauces.

More recently, KFC South Korea introduced a massive Doom Triple Down sandwich as part of its Avengers: Doomsday collaboration, stacking three pieces of fried chicken together without a traditional bun.

KFC reveals massive Doom Triple Down sandwich in another green Avengers: Doomsday menu

KFC has a wild Flamin’ Hot Cheetos menu with a dirty soda

KFC launches new chicken “burger” you can drench in cheese

For now, Colourful Cheezy Cheese is exclusive to Malaysia, with no announced plans to bring the rainbow-colored snack to restaurants in the United States or other markets.

KFC has also yet to confirm how long the colorful fried cheese will remain available.

The Justice and Commerce Departments have announced an investigation into the company previously run by the woman officials have accused of being a Chinese spy while having an affair with Jensen Huang while he was Nvidia's CEO.

A criminal investigation into Megaspeed has been confirmed by the United States Justice and Commerce Departments, with a joint statement saying that they will “bring the full extent of the law against Megaspeed for any criminal conduct that may have occurred.” Authorities believe that Megaspeed is one of a raft of companies that may have circumvented export rules designed to limit the sale of advanced AI chips, like those built by Nvidia, to approved companies and countries. The company is at the heart of the recent Jensen Huang scandal, which saw him resign from the company last week.

Megaspeed was added to Nvidia's no-sell list in June 2025, the statement reveals, but a New York Times report claims that a Malaysian Megaspeed subsidiary continued to receive large shipments of Nvidia chips for a full eight months after that date. It's believed that Nvidia's export-controlled Blackwell and H200 chips were shipped to Speedmatrix, either via third parties or directly from Nvidia itself, before being handed off to China. Nvidia has argued that it was given Commerce Department permission to ship the components to Megaspeed. Notably, Nvidia continues to list Megaspeed as a cloud partner.

The new criminal investigation follows a Commerce Department investigation into Megaspeed that first kicked off in 2024 over possible export violations. By May 2025, it had requested Nvidia's data relating to sales it made to Megaspeed, and by June, officials visited Megaspeed and even interviewed Alice Huang, the now-former executive who is allegedly a Chinese spy. Ms. Huang is also the woman that then-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang had an affair with before resigning his position earlier this month. Following that visit, the Justice Department took the lead in the investigation into Megaspeed.

Joint Statement from Department of Commerce and Department of Justice on Megaspeed Investigation. https://t.co/aAdtN9aDfFNovember 17, 2026

Megaspeed has long been accused of being a front for an AI component smuggling operation. In October 2025, an investigation claimed that it had helped move around $2 billion worth of Nvidia chips into China by importing them and then re-exporting them to the country. Megaspeed says that it complies with all laws and that the woman at the center of the affair scandal, Ms. Huang, is no longer at the company. The Chinese government has also dismissed claims that she was a state spy.

The joint Commerce and Justice Departments' statement pointed to its strong track record when prosecuting chip smugglers to date. 17 defendants across a total of six investigations have been charged so far, with more likely to follow. A Californian man was recently arrested after being accused of smuggling more than $300 million worth of export-restricted Nvidia chips to China. He now faces up to 20 years in prison.

#10573. Tiny nanolaser could cut computer energy use in half

The development of a nanolaser could mark an early step toward a future in which microchips communicate entirely with particles of light.

Researchers at DTU have created a tiny nanolaser that could eventually help make computers, smartphones, and data centers faster while using much less energy. The breakthrough was published in the scientific journalScience Advances.

In the future, thousands of these lasers could potentially be integrated onto a single microchip. Instead of moving information around a chip with electrical signals, devices could transmit data using photons, the particles that make up light.

"The nanolaser opens up the possibility of creating a new generation of components that combine high performance with minimal size. This could be in information technology, for example, where ultra-small and energy-efficient lasers can reduce energy consumption in computers, or in the development of sensors for the healthcare sector, where the nanolaser's extreme light concentration can deliver high-resolution images and ultrasensitive biosensors," says DTU professor Jesper Mørk.

Mørk co-authored the study with, among others, Drs. Meng Xiong and Yi Yu from DTU Electro.

Much of the internet already relies on light to carry information through fiber optic cables. Inside computers, however, data still moves through electronic circuits using electricity. That approach produces heat and can limit how quickly information can be transferred.

Nanolasers could bring optical communication directly onto microchips. By generating light signals efficiently inside the chip, they could allow information to move with very little energy loss, potentially making future devices faster, cooler, and more energy efficient.

Mørk estimates that using nanolasers in computers could reduce energy consumption by as much as half.

The compact DTU nanolaser is an important step toward that goal. Future chips designed around light-based communication would likely require thousands of extremely small, efficient lasers working together to transmit information across the chip.

The new nanolaser was created in DTU's clean room facility, DTU Nanolab. According to Mørk, the device pushes beyond the conventional limit for how small a laser can be made.

At its core is a structure known as a nanocavity, which traps and concentrates light within an exceptionally tiny space. Until now, achieving such intense confinement at this scale had been considered extremely difficult.

When researchers shine a beam of light onto the device, both photons and electrons become concentrated in the same microscopic region. This interaction allows the laser to function at room temperature while requiring unusually little energy.

The light-trapping structure used in the nanolaser was originally developed by Professor Ole Sigmund's group at DTU Construct.

The next major challenge is making the nanolaser operate using electrical power. If researchers can achieve that, the technology could have broad applications across computing, communications, and healthcare.

Computers and smartphones could potentially deliver greater performance while consuming less electricity. Data centers, which require enormous amounts of power, could also see substantial reductions in energy use, potentially leading to significant climate benefits.

In healthcare technology, the nanolaser's ability to concentrate light into an extremely small area could support ultra-sensitive sensors and high-resolution imaging systems.

Researchers estimate that the remaining technical challenges could be solved within the next 5-10 years.

Materials provided by Technical University of Denmark. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
